## Page 1


INTEGRAL HAMILTONIAN FLOER THEORY: FOUNDATIONS
SHAOYUN BAI AND GUANGBO XU
Abstract. Continuing our previous work on the integral Arnold conjecture [BX22a], we
establish the foundation of Hamiltonian Floer theory over integer coefficients on any compact
symplectic manifold. This package produces a well-defined chain homotopy class of Floer
complexes, chain-level continuation maps, the Piunikhin–Salamon–Schwarz (PSS) isomorphism,
and an associative pair-of-pants product. When the Hamiltonian is the p-th iteration for a
prime number p, we also establish the Zp-equivariant Floer theory in characteristic p, including
the equivariant Floer complex and the Zp-equivariant pair-of-pants product. Moreover, we
define quantum Steenrod operations for any compact symplectic manifold and compare them
with the equivariant pair-of-pants products via the Zp-equivariant PSS map. We also obtain
chain-level invariants such as spectral numbers and barcodes and prove quantitative properties
of the equivariant pair-of-pants product.
The counting theory is based on Fukaya–Ono’s normally polynomial perturbation scheme
[FO97] and its realization by the authors in [BX22b], termed FOP perturbations, adapted in
the abstract setting of flow categories, flow multimodules, and homotopies of flow bimodules.
Along the way, we construct global Kuranishi charts for the relevant moduli spaces following
Abouzaid–McLean–Smith’s framework and its adaptation to the Hamiltonian Floer setting due
to the authors, which may be of independent interest.

1. Introduction
Floer theory is one of the most influential discoveries in geometry and topology. In symplectic
topology, the Hamiltonian Floer homology [Flo89] provides a powerful framework for studying
Hamiltonian dynamics. Most notably, it leads to proofs of various versions of the famous Arnold
conjecture [Arn65]. Several variations, such as quantitative Floer theory and local Floer theory,
played important roles in many other applications in Hamiltonian dynamics.
The development of Floer homology was accompanied by the advancement of the understanding
of pseudoholomorphic curves [Gro85] and regularizations of their moduli spaces.
In earlier
works [Flo89][HS95][Ono95] where certain vanishing or positivity conditions were assumed, when
defining the Floer complexes, one only needs to consider moduli spaces of virtual dimensions
at most one; one can achieve transversality of such moduli spaces by perturbing geometrically


## Page 3


INTEGRAL HAMILTONIAN FLOER THEORY
3
and can obtain Floer complexes with coefficients in Z.
When the semipositivity condition
was removed, there is no known way to achieve transversality via geometric perturbations
without imposing further conditions (cf. [CM07]). The abstract perturbation methods used in
[LT98b][FO99][HWZ21][Par16a] require one to regularize the moduli spaces via Kuranishi models
or polyfolds and to apply virtual perturbations or integrations. One then has to deal with moduli
spaces of negative virtual dimensions and to organize all the constructions in a compatible way
which can be formalized using Cohen–Jones–Segal’s notion of flow categories [CJS95]. The lack of
geometric transversality is closely intertwined with the orbispace structure of the moduli spaces,
which naturally leads to fractional counts of curves and chain-level structures only defined over Q.
The main purpose of the current work is to extend Floer theory from rational to integer coefficients
for general closed symplectic manifolds.
In [BX22a] we have already established a portion
of the integral Floer theory which provides a proof of the Arnold conjecture over Z.
The
motivation to go beyond the scope of [BX22a], besides a complete package of the integral
Hamiltonian Floer theory, resides in the potential applications in Hamiltonian dynamics. For this
purpose, we will construct quantum Steenrod operations for general symplectic manifolds (see
[Wil20][SW22][Sei15a][SZ21] under semipositivity or stronger assumptions). In an upcoming joint
work with Shelukhin and Wilkins [BSWX26], we will extend many dynamical applications (see
[She20][She21][She22][CGG22][AL25]) of quantum Steenrod operations to greater generality.
The integral extension is possible due to the recent developments in the virtual technique. The most
crucial idea was given by Fukaya–Ono [FO97]. Roughly speaking, the moduli spaces carry natural
stable complex structures, which allow a specific type of single-valued perturbations (normally
polynomial perturbations) resulting in well-defined integer counts. Fukaya–Ono discussed the
original idea for a local Kuranishi model, while B. Parker [Par13] further explored strategies to
globalize. In [BX22b] we provide a rigorous treatment of Fukaya–Ono’s proposal and define the
so-called “FOP transversality condition” for normally polynomial perturbations.
Another crucial concurrent development is the Abouzaid–McLean–Smith construction of global
Kuranishi charts (see [AMS21, HS22, AMS23]). This new technique helps avoid the complicated
inductive arguments involving multiple charts (such as having good coordinate systems [FOOO16]).
Meanwhile, the smoothing argument of [AMS21] replaces the hard analytic approach [FOOO24]
by a soft topological approach. These novel ideas helped significantly in the case of Hamiltonian
Floer theory. One of the main challenges in Floer theory is to carry out AMS construction for
infinitely many moduli spaces compatibly.
We hope that the foundational work laid in this paper can serve as a useful package for applications
of Floer theory to Hamiltonian dynamics on general compact symplectic manifolds. Our joint
paper with Shelukhin and Wilkins [BSWX26] showcases how the technical work done here can
efficiently lead to interesting statements for Hamiltonian maps on general compact symplectic
manifolds. Meanwhile, we hope the technical procedures can serve as a model to be adopted in
other situations such as in Lagrangian Floer theory or contact topology.
Our main results fall into three parts:
(1) Integral Hamiltonian Floer theory at the chain level (Theorems A—G).
(2) Equivariant Floer theory and quantum Steenrod operations (Theorems H—J).
(3) Quantitative refinements, including spectral invariants and barcodes (Theorems K—M)
as well as results about Floer complexes of homologically nontrivial orbits (Theorem 1.11
and Theorem 1.12).
Below we state these main results in a way that readers can directly cite.

coefficients, which is well-defined up to chain homotopy equivalence. Under certain restrictions of
the Hamiltonian, such a complex was constructed by the authors [BX22a] without proving the
homotopy equivalence between different choices.

Theorem F. The PSS map on homology is an isomorphism of graded ΛΠ-modules.
Remark 1.1. We point out that we do not know if the PSS and the SSP maps are inverses to each
other up to homotopy. In particular, the proof of Theorem F involves new moduli spaces beyond
those appearing in the constructions of Theorem E.
Of course, the Arnold conjecture over integer coefficients now follows from Theorem F as a
corollary, although in [BX22a] we proved it by only considering the Floer chain complex for special
H and proving only the injectivity part of Theorem F.
Corollary 1.2 (Integral Arnold conjecture, cf. [BX22a]). Let ϕ : X →X be a nondegenerate
Hamiltonian diffeomorphism. Let N be the minimal Chern number of (X, ω). Then the number of
fixed points of ϕ is no less than the minimal number of generators of a Z/2N-graded complex of
free abelian groups whose homology is isomorphic to H∗(X; Z) viewed as a Z/2N-graded abelian
group.
If we compose the Floer-theoretic Poincar´e pairing with the PSS map, then it induces a nonde-
generate bilinear pairing on the homology H∗(X; ΛΠ).
Definition 1.3. The FOP Poincar´e pairing on H(X; ΛΠ) is the bilinear pairing
⟨ΦPSS(·), ΦPSS(·)⟩: H(X; ΛΠ) ⊗
ΛΠH(X; ΛΠ) →ΛΠ.
If we use the PSS map defined using the technology of [FO99][LT98b][Par16a], then over rational
coefficients, such a pairing coincides with the classical Poincar´e pairing on homology. This
is because the orbifold Euler classes satisfy properties needed for S1-localization, so there is
no contribution from moduli spaces of curves with nonzero energy. However, the FOP Euler
characteristic classes arising in our perturbation scheme do not necessarily satisfy these properties
and we do not know if the pairing is classical or not. This phenomenon is similar to the case
of quantum K-theory (see [Lee04]) or quantum generalized cohomology developed by Abouzaid–
McLean–Smith [AMS23]. The point of introducing the quantum-corrected Poincar´e pairing is to
define an associative product analogous to the quantum product on H∗(X; ΛΠ) in our context,
where we use the pairing to turn three-pointed Gromov–Witten type counts into a bilinear
operation on H∗(X; ΛΠ) to dualize a marked point into an output marked point.
Another corollary is the invertibility of the Seidel map with integer coefficients defined in [BPX24].
Recall that for each loop of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms ϕ : S1 →Ham(X), one has a Hamiltonian
fibration f

1.1.5. Comparison with known construction. When (X, ω) is weakly monotone (also called semi-
positive), then by perturbing either the almost complex structure J or the Hamiltonian H,
Hofer–Salamon [HS95] and Ono [Ono95] defined the Floer chain complex over integers. Such
constructions can also be carried out to define continuation maps, pair-of-pants products, and
K¨unneth isomorphisms with integer coefficients for weakly monotone (X, ω). It is natural to
wonder if our constructions agree with the classical definitions in the weakly monotone setting.
As a proof of principle, we demonstrate that this is the case for the Floer homology.
Theorem G. When (X, ω) is weakly monotone, our Floer chain complex CF∗(H, J, Ξ) is chain
homotopy equivalent to the one defined using the classical method.
We expect that the proof methods can be generalized to establish the identification between our
Floer-theoretic invariants and the classical versions.

1.2. Main results on equivariant Floer theory and Steenrod operations. To discuss
Steenrod operations and other aspects of equivariant Floer theory, we switch to the cohomological
convention, partially to match with the fact that classical Steenrod operations are cohomological
operations. In this way, by considering ascending flows rather than descending flows, one obtains
the Floer cochain complex CF ∗(H, J, Ξ) associated to a Floer flow category whose cochains are
formal infinite linear combinations of capped 1-periodic orbits of H whose actions go to +∞.
Let p be a prime number. Abbreviate Zp := Z/pZ. When we view it as a field serving as the
coefficient field of cohomology, we use Fp instead. In characteristic p, on any topological space,
there are the Steenrod power operations which can be organized as a single nonlinear map.

Remark 1.6. There are two main reasons why we prefer the Tate version. First, all the nice
properties of the equivariant Floer theory hold for the Tate version but not the Borel one, for
example, localization. Second, the inversion of the variable U follows more naturally from our
geometric construction. The Borel version, instead, is regarded as a truncation of the Tate version.
From now on, all equivariant cochain complexes or cohomology, without further declaration, will
be the Tate version.

1.2.1. The quantum Steenrod operations. The classical Steenrod square resp. power operations
are cohomological operations which can be formulated as a single map (for p = 2)
St2 : H∗(X; F2) →H∗(X; F2[θ]) ⊆H∗(X; eKp)
resp. (p > 2)
Stp : H∗(X; Fp) →H∗(X; Fp[U]⟨θ⟩) ⊆H∗(X; eKp).
The map Stp is additive and linear over Fp. Moreover, if we regard U of degree 2 and θ of degree
1, then Stp multiplies the grading by p. Fukaya proposed the idea of defining quantum Steenrod
operation [Fuk97]. It has been adapted in the Floer setting by Seidel [Sei15a] and Shelukhin–Zhao
[SZ21] and in the quantum cohomology setting by Wilkins [Wil20] and Seidel–Wilkins [SW22]. A
key point in the construction is the integrality of curve counts, which then admit well-defined
mod p reduction. Hence the existing constructions are all restricted to monotone or semipositive
settings. The FOP perturbation method developed in [BX22b] allows one to extend to the general
setting.

SHAOYUN BAI AND GUANGBO XU

symplectic manifold (X, ω), for each prime p, there are well-defined additive maps

1.3.4. Quantitative Hamiltonian Floer theory for noncontractible orbits. We state the main quan-
titative results about Floer theory for noncontractible orbits. We follow very much the treatment
of Sugimoto [Sug26]. Since the resulting homology group always vanishes, there are no spectral
invariants; hence the barcodes are the main quantitative aspects here.

[γ] ̸= 0 ∈H1(X; Z)free.

Definition 1.10. Let H be a 1-periodic Hamiltonian on (X, ω).
(1) Let x : S1 →X be a 1-periodic orbit of H. A γ-capping of x is an oriented cobordism
u : Σ →X from γ to x (where Σ could be a higher genus surface).
(2) Two γ-cappings are equivalent if their difference, which is a class in H2(X; Z), has zero
symplectic area. A γ-capped orbit of H is a 1-periodic orbit together with an equivalence
class of γ-cappings.

1.4. What we do “virtually”. We would like to explain the general principle of our construction
(on the virtual side) along with some historical remarks.
In a very general sense, the Floer complexes and other chain-level structures are constructed via
counting solutions to the Floer equation. To count beyond the cases where transversality can
be achieved by geometric perturbations ([Flo89][HS95][Ono95]), the so-called virtual technique
was invented (see [LT98a][FO99][FOOO20][HWZ21][Par16a] etc.). Such constructions, depending
on the extent of applications, usually require building a complete package of abstract theory
(such as Kuranishi theory and polyfold theory); the application of these abstract theories can
typically be broken down into two main components: (virtual) regularization and (virtual)
perturbation/integration.
From now on we only discuss under the finite-dimensional/Kuranishi approach, the one upon
which the current paper is based, rather than the infinite-dimensional/polyfold approach. The
issues and challenges we will address in the finite-dimensional approach may not be manifested in
the infinite-dimensional situation.
A central issue in the finite-dimensional construction is the choice-dependence.
Unlike the
algebro-geometric situation, one rarely obtains canonical objects such as a perfect obstruction
theory ([BF97]). To obtain choice-independent results, one needs to include various kinds of
equivalence relations in the abstract package, or perhaps higher categorical structures. The
choice-independence is not yet a significant challenge in cases where one deals with a single or
finitely many moduli spaces, such as Gromov–Witten theory. But it causes much more serious
difficulties in Floer-type theories. In Floer theory, essentially one obtains virtual fundamental
chains rather than cycles; the virtual fundamental chains satisfy expected properties (such as
d2 = 0) only if we make the constructions for infinitely many moduli spaces compatibly. It
could be a delicate issue when developing the abstract package incorporating such compatibility
conditions and when fulfilling these conditions in the concrete regularization procedure.5
We would like to emphasize two important developments which precede the current piece of work.
The first one, regarding the perturbation component, starts from [FO97] which proposed the
principle leading to integer-valued curve counts even when the orbifold locus of a moduli space is
obstructed. After technical exploration by Parker [Par13], the authors rigorously established this
new perturbation method in [BX22b], which we call the FOP perturbation scheme. The second
one, regarding the regularization component, is the construction of global Kuranishi charts starting
from [AMS21]. It provides a powerful method producing global Kuranishi models regularizing
any single moduli space and greatly reduces the technicality when discussing the compatibility
among local Kuranishi charts. In [BX22a] we produced the global Kuranishi chart construction
for Floer complexes and PSS maps, which is being extended to other chain-level structures in the
current paper (with certain upgrades coming from [AMS23] and [HS22]).
Now we summarize the technical framework underlying our implementation of the virtual technique.
Compared to the regularization component, the perturbation component is to simply apply a
blackbox theorem (Theorem N) which was already established in [BX22b]. However, the delicate
prerequisite for the blackbox theorem, along with other requirements, demands careful fine-tunings
5The work of Abouzaid [Abo22] takes a different approach where the choices are made universally in his notion
of “theories of virtual counts.”


## Page 17


INTEGRAL HAMILTONIAN FLOER THEORY
17
of the regularization component. Since each stage produces different kinds of objects whose
compatibility conditions all look similar to flow category structure, each stage-wise construction
is phrased as producing a flow category enriched in a particular “regular stratification category.”
The objects of such a category are stratified in a way similar to manifolds with corners, with a
monoidal structure corresponding to (direct) product and disjoint union.
To be more concrete, the whole procedure we are describing can be summarized in the following
zig-zag of functors
regularization
z
}|
{
Top ←SKurNC →dOrbNC ←dOrbFOP →ΨMan
|
{z
}
perturbation
.
The items are all regular stratification categories where
• Top is the category of stratified topological spaces;
• SKurNC is the category of normally complex smooth Kuranishi charts;
• dOrbNC is the category of normally complex derived orbifolds;
• ΨMan is the category of pseudomanifolds.
In the case of Floer complex, the geometric data of Floer theory (the Hamiltonian and the almost
complex structure) provides a flow category enriched in Top. The regularization component can
be summarized as constructing a “lift” of this flow category to SKurNC. This step is basically
accomplished by repackaging the AMS construction [AMS21, AMS23] (where the construction of
the normal complex structure also borrows ideas from [AB24]), which contains several delicate
steps. Once this lift is constructed, it automatically descends to a flow category enriched in
dOrbNC, where the blackbox theorem on FOP perturbations can be directly applied. After the
FOP perturbation, the flow category becomes enriched in ΨMan, which automatically produces
a chain complex over integers.
To obtain chain-level structures such as continuation maps, pair-of-pants products etc., we also
consider two “higher” analogues of flow categories, i.e., flow multimodules and flow homotopies.
Typically, flow categories induce chain complexes, multimodules induce chain maps, and flow
homotopies induce chain homotopies. These chain-level structures are also obtained from compat-
ibly implementing the AMS+FOP procedure, namely, producing multimodules and homotopies
enriched in categories appearing in the above zig-zag diagram. The differences from the flow
category cases only lie in various sub-technical levels.
1.4.1. Other AMS-type regularization schemes. There have been several related works which carry
out AMS-type constructions in Floer theory, which aim at constructing not only a single global
chart construction, but a compatible system for all involved moduli spaces. These works include
[Rez22], [HH25], [CH25], [MSS25], and [Rab25], as well as our previous work [BX22a].
One distinct feature of our work is the formal description of the compatibility condition using the
notion of “lifts” of flow categories to various “regular stratification categories.” Having realized
that each step of the AMS construction requires a certain compatibility for the choices made
for individual moduli spaces (such as the thickening data, stable smoothing, normal complex
structure etc.), the formal notions of regular stratification categories and lifts are more convenient
and rigorous, although one needs to carefully define each regular stratification category.
The compatibility condition we require here is also most refined since we need to construct
genuine perturbations (essentially the same level of refinedness should be established in [Rez22]
and [Rab25], both of which use the FOP perturbation method developed by us). The specific
requirement is contained in the definition of various regular stratification categories, most notably


## Page 18


18
SHAOYUN BAI AND GUANGBO XU
⌊SKurNC
rig , the category of collared rigidified normally complex smooth Kuranishi spaces. Here the
collaredness and rigidifiedness are what make the inductive perturbation possible, from low-energy
moduli spaces to high-energy ones. Some other works such as [HH25] and [CH25] where genuine
perturbations are not needed, the requirement of compatibility can be relaxed. On the other hand,
the work [MSS25], which also relies on constructing compatible (multi-valued) perturbations, uses
a different strategy to interpolate not-so-compatible choices over the collar regions.
1.5. Structure of the paper. This paper is divided into four main parts. In Part 1, we introduce
a series of formal notions, including various categories such as those of pseudomanifolds with
corners, Kuranishi spaces, derived orbifolds, and their variants; the notions of flow categories,
multimodules, and homotopies; the abstract construction of chain-level objects; and the blackbox of
FOP perturbation method. In Part 2, we introduce flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies
appearing in (non-equivariant) Floer theory, state the main technical theorems about AMS
regularization, and prove most of the main results stated in the introduction. In Part 3, we
provide the details of the AMS construction asserted in Part 2. In Part 4, we modify all the
previous constructions in the equivariant setting and establish results related to the quantum
Steenrod operations.
1.6. Acknowledgements. We thank Egor Shelukhin and Nicolas Wilkins for related discussions
involving our collaboration [BSWX26]. We thank Zihong Chen, Helmut Hofer, Rohil Prasad,
Sobhan Seyfaddini for their interest in our work and encouragement.


## Page 19


INTEGRAL HAMILTONIAN FLOER THEORY
19
Part 1. FORMAL NOTIONS
Outline of Part 1
We introduce the abstract framework underlying the formulation of Floer theory in various levels.
Several concepts related to Floer theory and the virtual construction are described in more abstract
ways, including the abstract notion of flow categories/multimodules/homotopies/concatenations
enriched in so-called regular stratification categories and their lifts to different categories. Concrete
examples of such regular stratification categories will appear in the geometric construction,
including Kuranishi spaces, derived orbifolds, pseudomanifolds, etc. We also formulate the abstract
mechanism for deriving chain-level structures from flow categories/multimodules/homotopies.
Lastly, we provide the FOP perturbation method as a blackbox theorem which can be directly
applied.
2. Regular Stratification Categories
2.1. Regular posets. The purpose of this subsection is to introduce notions associated with
partially ordered sets which will be used to define the index sets of stratifications of various moduli
spaces coming from geometric constructions.
2.1.1. Partially ordered sets. The term “partially ordered set” is abbreviated as poset, which is
always assumed to be countable. The partial order relations are denoted by ≤, ⪯, etc. A poset
map is a map of the underlying sets which respects the partial orders.
For a poset A, let Amax = Atop ⊆A be the subset of maximal elements. A poset A has a canonical
Alexandrov topology: a subset U ⊆A is Alexandrov open if α ∈U and α ≤β imply that
β ∈U. By definition, poset maps are continuous under the Alexandrov topology. A special class
of closed subset indexed by α ∈A
∂αA = {β ∈A | β ≤α}
is used frequently in this paper.
Remark 2.1. Our convention is opposite from that of Abouzaid [Abo22], where he regards top
strata as minimal elements of the labelling poset.
The product of finitely many posets carries a canonically induced partial order from the lexico-
graphic order. Indeed, if A1, . . . , Ak are posets, then the relation
(α1, . . . , αk) ≤(β1, . . . , βk) if and only if αi ≤βi ∀i = 1, . . . , k,
is a partial order on A1 × · · · × Ak.
Notation 2.2. Let S be a countable subset. Let A(S) denote the set of all finite subsets of S whose
partial order is α ≤β =⇒β ⊆α. So its maximal element is the empty set.
Now we introduce the kind of posets which can model manifolds with corners.
Definition 2.3. (Homogeneous posets)
(1) A poset A is called homogeneous if for each α ∈A, the length of a maximal sequence of
elements α = α0 < α1 < · · · < αk with αk ∈Amax, is finite and only depends on α (but
not the maximal string and αk). This length is called the depth or codimension of α,
denoted by depth(α). In particular,
α ∈Amax ⇐⇒depth(α) = 0.

We often need to identify the faces that are only adjacent to one top stratum.
Definition 2.5. Let A be a regular poset.
(1) A true boundary stratum of A is an element β ∈A of depth 1 which has only one top
stratum above. The true boundary of A is the Alexandrov closure of the set of true
boundary strata.
(2) A regular poset is called normal if each depth-one element is either adjacent to one top
stratum (true boundary) or adjacent to two top strata (fake boundary).
Remark 2.6. The poset A(S) in Notation 2.2 is regular with depth function given by depth(α) = #α.
On the other hand, the poset of three elements α < β < γ is homogeneous but not regular.
We prove several important properties of regular posets.

It is straightforward to check that regular posets admit finite products (Cartesian product) and
finite coproducts (disjoint union).
Definition 2.8. The category of regular posets, denoted by RegPos, has objects being countable
regular posets and morphisms being injective homogeneous poset maps.
Proposition 2.9. RegPos is a distributive monoidal category with respect to the direct product
and disjoint union, whose units are posets with a single element.
Proof. This is straightforward from checking the definitions.
□
2.2. Regular stratification categories and presentations.
Definition 2.10. A regular stratification category is a distributive monoidal category C
together with a distributive monoidal functor C →RegPos, called the forgetful functor,
satisfying the following properties. In the following, we say an object K of C an A-stratified
object if the forgetful functor sends K to A.
(1) There is an initial object which is ∅-stratified.
(2) For any morphism f : A →B in RegPos and any B-stratified object K, there is a
canonical “pullback,” which is an A-stratified object f ∗K, together with a canonical
morphism ˜f : f ∗K →K. The pullback needs to satisfy the functoriality condition.
Namely, the composition of pullbacks is the pullback by the composition.
In particular, for any A-stratified object K and α ∈A there is a restriction ∂αK for
each α ∈A.


## Page 22

SHAOYUN BAI AND GUANGBO XU
As we will see below, there are a few examples of regular stratification categories such as those of
regularly stratified topological spaces (with morphisms being embeddings), regularly stratified
topological/smooth manifolds, regularly stratified Kuranishi charts, and Kuranishi presentations.
On the other hand, if C is any category, then the product RegPos × C is a regular stratification
category with respect to the obvious forgetful functor.
2.3. Stratified spaces, manifolds, and pseudomanifolds.
2.3.1. Regularly stratified spaces. Although we can define stratified spaces in general, in this paper
all such spaces have strata indexed by regular posets. Therefore, we restrict to this situation. We
will specify a regular stratification category Top of regularly stratified topological spaces.
Definition 2.11. Let A be a countable regular poset. An A-stratified space (A-space for short)
is a locally compact, Hausdorff and second countable topological space X endowed with a map
s : X →A
which is continuous with respect to the Alexandrov topology on A whose range is finite.

(2) A stratified map f : X1 →X2 as above is called an embedding if f is a homeomorphism

(3) The category Top is the category of topological spaces stratified by regular posets whose
morphisms are stratified embeddings.
(4) Let G be a topological group. A G-action on an A-space X is a continuous G-action on X
which preserves each stratum. In this case, the ∂αA-space ∂αX has an induced G-action.

[0, ϵ)S = {(xa)a∈S | xa ∈[0, ϵ)}
which is stratified by A(S).

2.3.3. Stratified pseudomanifolds.
Definition 2.15. An n-dimensional pseudomanifold is a topological space M with a partition
M = IntM ⊔δM satisfying
(1) IntM is open, dense, and itself an n-dimensional topological manifold.
(2) δM admits a partition into topological manifolds of dimensions at most n −2.
Definition 2.16. Let A be a countable regular poset. An A-stratified n-dimensional pseudo-
manifold is an A-stratified space X together with partitions Xα = IntXα ⊔δXα such that
(1) For each α ∈A, Xα = IntXα ⊔δXα is a pseudomanifold of dimension n −depth(α).
7It follows from this condition that each ∂αX is a topological manifold with boundary whose interior is Xα.

Notice that a stratified zero-dimensional pseudomanifold is a discrete collection of points and a
stratified 1-dimensional pseudomanifold is a disjoint union of 1-manifolds (with boundary).
Definition 2.17. An embedding of stratified pseudomanifolds from X1 to X2 is an embedding of
stratified spaces f : X1 →X2 such that f(IntX1) ⊆Int(X2) and f(δX1) ⊆δX2. The category of
stratified pseudomanifolds, denoted by ΨMan, has objects being stratified pseudomanifolds and
morphisms being embeddings.
One can check that ΨMan is a regular stratification category (Definition 2.10).
3. Kuranishi Spaces
The Kuranishi theory for moduli spaces of pseudoholomorphic curves originated from the efforts of
extending the definition of symplectic Gromov–Witten invariants and Hamiltonian Floer homology
to full generality. It is based on the fundamental fact that the Cauchy–Riemann operator is
elliptic. Fukaya–Ono [FO99] firstly introduced the package of Kuranishi structures, alongside
another finite-dimensional reduction approach of Li–Tian [LT98a] (see also [Sie96] [Rua99]). While
the method of Kuranishi structures was further developed by Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono (see the
comprehensive treatment in [FOOO20]) together with closely related works by McDuff–Wehrheim
[MW18, MW17a, MW17b], there are alternate approaches with considerable novelties, including
the algebraic version of Pardon [Par16a] as well as the more recent “global chart construction” of
Abouzaid–McLean–Smith [AMS21] (followed up by [HS22] and [AMS23]).
The AMS construction was adopted by the authors in [BX22b] in the Gromov–Witten setting
and [BX22a] in the Hamiltonian Floer setting.
We use Kur, SKur, and SKurNC to denote the category of Kuranishi charts in topological,
smooth, or stably complex categories. When the discussion can be unified in the three cases, we
simply use Kur. See below for the detailed definitions.
3.1. Kuranishi spaces.
Definition 3.1. Let A be a countable regular poset.
(1) An A-stratified (topological) Kuranishi space (K-space for short) is a quadruple
K = (G, V, E, S) where G is a compact Lie group, V is an A-manifold with a continuous
G-action, E →V is a G-equivariant vector bundle, and S : V →E is a G-equivariant
section, such that the G-action on V has only finite stabilizers.
(2) A Kuranishi space K = (G, V, E, S) is said to be smooth if V is a smooth A-manifold,
the G-action is smooth, and E →V is a smooth equivariant vector bundle (we do not
impose any smoothness condition on S).
3.1.1. Morphisms of Kuranishi spaces. In order to define a regular stratification category (see
Definition 2.10), we need to specify morphisms. We first define a general form of maps.

to K2 = (G2, V2, E2, S2), denoted by ι21 : K1 →K2, consists of a Lie group homomorphism

Remark 3.8. The above definition basically says that a morphism ι21 : K1 →K2 is an embedding
if (up to group enlargement) the domain map is an embedding and the restriction of K2 to a
neighborhood of the embedding image is isomorphic to a stabilization. One can also define a
weaker “infinitesimal version” of K-chart embeddings. In the smooth category one can prove that
the infinitesimal version is equivalent to the above definition. However, in the topological category
it may not be so.
One can check that compositions of embeddings are still embeddings, which ultimately follows
from the fact that every G′-equivariant vector bundle over G′/G for a group embedding G ,→G′
is induced from a G-representation. Also notice that stabilization maps, open embeddings, and
group enlargements are all special cases of embeddings.
Definition 3.9. The categories of regularly stratified topological and smooth Kuranishi spaces,
denoted by Kur and SKur, are the categories whose objects are stratified Kuranishi charts in
the topological and smooth categories and whose morphisms are embeddings.
Proposition 3.10. Kur and SKur are regular stratification categories.
Proof. Disjoint union and direct product make the category Kur distributive monoidal, with
monoidal unit being the singleton K0 = ({0}, pt, 0, 0). On the other hand, one can obviously
take pullbacks of any A-stratified Kuranishi space with respect to homogeneous poset maps
f : B →A.
□
3.1.2. Free quotients. There is another relation among Kuranishi spaces which will appear in the
construction. This notion first appeared as group enlargement in [AMS21, Section 4] and then
showed up as free quotient in [AMS23, Lemma 3.5].
Definition 3.11. Let K∼= (G∼, V ∼, E∼, S∼) be a (topological or smooth) Kuranishi space
where G∼= G × G′ is the product of two compact Lie groups. Suppose the G′-action on V ∼is
free. Then the free quotient of K∼by G′ is the Kuranishi space
K := K/G′ := (G, V ∼/G′, E∼/G′, S∼/G′).
Notice that there is a natural strict morphism (which is not an embedding) from K∼to K. We
also call the morphism a free quotient by G′.

3.3. Stable complex structures and normal complex structures. Next, we discuss stable
and normal complex structures on Kuranishi spaces. Geometrically, such structures arise from
index-theoretic considerations of (virtual) tangent bundles of moduli spaces of pseudoholomorphic
curves. Such structures are also instrumental for incorporating the FOP perturbation scheme to
define integral invariants.
3.3.1. Stable morphisms of vector bundles. We start with a few basic definitions.
Definition 3.16. Let G be a Lie group, V be a G-space, and E →V be an equivariant vector
bundle.
(1) Let F →V be another G-equivariant vector bundle. A G-equivariant stable (iso)morphism
from E to F is an equivalence class of triples (R−, R+, τ) where R± are a pair of real
vector spaces (viewed as trivial representations of G) and
τ : R−⊕E →R+ ⊕F
is a G-equivariant (iso)morphism of vector bundles. The equivalence relation is induced by
(R−, R+, τ) ∼(R−⊕W, R+ ⊕W, τ ⊕IdW ) where W is another real vector space. Denote
a stable (iso)morphism by
τ : E
s→F.
(2) If E, F are complex vector bundles, then a stable (iso)morphism τ : E
s→F is called
complex if it is represented by a triple (R−, R+, τ) where R± are complex vector spaces
and τ is a complex vector bundle map.
One can verify that stable morphisms can be composed and stable isomorphisms admit inverses
as stable isomorphisms.
3.3.2. Stable complex structures.

Definition 3.20. A strict embedding of stably complex Kuranishi spaces from K1 to K2 consists

ιa : K1 →K3 is provided by a disk bundle in a complex vector bundle and the open embedding
ιc : K4 →K2 is an open embedding of stably complex Kuranishi spaces.
An embedding of stably complex Kuranishi spaces is a conjugacy class of strict embeddings.
Lemma 3.21. (Strict) embeddings of stably complex Kuranishi spaces can be composed.
Proof. Essentially one needs to consider disk bundles over disk bundles to ensure the compatibility
of stable complex structures. Given Ehresmann connections on D21 →V1 and D32 →V2, the
horizontal distribution of D21 can then be lifted to D32, giving a Ehresmann connection on the
iterated bundle.

Remark 3.22. We could define the category

whose objects are regularly stratified stably complex (in particular, smooth) Kuranishi spaces and
whose morphisms are embeddings. It is easy to see that this is a regular stratification category,
and there is a natural forgetful functor
SKurC →SKur.
One can also consider the rigidified version. However, it is rather complicated to obtain stably
complex Kuranishi spaces in our construction (although we could). Below we consider a more
flexible one using normal complex structures, which suffices for our purpose.
3.3.3. Normally complex Kuranishi spaces. Moduli spaces of pseudoholomorphic curves admit
stable complex structure because of the nature of the Cauchy–Riemann operator. In Section 16
we will follow the strategy of Abouzaid–Blumberg [AB21, AB24] to do the explicit construction.
However, it is a bit cumbersome to construct stable complex structures compatibly. Instead, we
will only construct the normal complex structure which is sufficient for our applications and where
the compatibility is easier to achieve.

Definition 3.24. A normally complex Kuranishi space (NC Kuranishi space for short) is a
smooth Kuranishi space K = (G, V, E, S) equipped with a G-invariant complex structure on E and
a G-invariant normal complex structure on TV/g. An open embedding of NC Kuranishi spaces
from K1 = (G1, V1, E1, S1) to K2 = (G2, V2, E2, S2) is a smooth open embedding ι21 : K1 →K2
such that 1) the vector bundle isomorphism
dι21 : TV1/g1 →TV2/g2
is normally complex and 2) the vector bundle isomorphism ιE
21 : E1 →E2 is complex-linear.
One can again discuss stabilizations (by complex vector bundles), strict embeddings, conjugations,
and embeddings of normally complex Kuranishi spaces. Then one obtains a regular stratification
category called the category of normally complex Kuranishi spaces, denoted by
SKurNC
and the corresponding rigidified category SKurNC

4. Derived Orbifolds
In this section we describe another regular stratification category called the category of derived
orbifolds. We will consider as general as the smooth category, but not the topological category.
4.1. Stratified orbifolds. All orbifolds, unless declared, are assumed to be smooth and effective.
They are spaces which are locally modelled by U/Γ where Γ is a finite group and U is an effective
smooth Γ-manifold. Orbifolds with boundary and corners are locally modelled on U/Γ where U is
a Γ-manifold with boundary and corners. If A is a regular poset (Definition 2.4), then one can
consider the notion of A-orbifold. Without giving the detailed definition, we remind the reader
that a smooth effective A-orbifold contains charts of the form
Uα × [0, ϵ)(Faceγ
α), where α ≤γ ∈Amax,
where Uα is a Γ-manifold without boundary. Here we always assume that the isotropy group
acts trivially on the normal factors. For example, if X is a smooth A-manifold acted on by a
compact Lie group G with finite stabilizers, then the quotient X/G is naturally a smooth effective
A-orbifold.
Definition 4.1 (Derived orbifolds). Fix a regular poset A. An A-stratified derived orbifold
is a triple D = (U, E, S) where U is an effective A-orbifold, E →U is a smooth orbifold vector
bundle, and S : U →E is a continuous section. A derived orbifold D is called compact if the
coarse space of S−1(0) is a compact topological space.
Similar to the case of Kuranishi spaces, we define embeddings of derived orbifolds as combinations
of several more basic morphisms.
Definition 4.2. Let D be an A-stratified derived orbifold.

(3) Given a derived orbifold D = (U, E, S), for any open subset U′ ⊆U with S−1(0) ⊆U′, the
shrinking of D to U′, denoted by D|U′, is the derived orbifold (U′, E|U′, S|U′). It admits
a natural open embedding into D.
(4) A disk bundle over an orbifold is an open neighborhood of the zero section of an orbifold
vector bundle. A stabilization of a derived orbifold D = (U, E, S) by a disk bundle
N →U contained in a vector bundle πF : F →U is the derived orbifold

(5) An embedding of derived orbifolds from D1 = (U1, E1, S1) to D2 = (U2, E2, S2) is a
morphism ϕ21 : D1 →D2 such that there exists a disk bundle N21 →U1 and an open
embedding θ21 : StabN21(D1) →D2 extending ϕ21.
Definition 4.3. The category of stratified derived orbifolds, denoted by dOrb, has objects being
stratified derived orbifolds and morphisms being embeddings of derived orbifolds.
The following is readily checked.
Proposition 4.4. The category dOrb is a regular stratification category (Definition 2.10).
□
One can see that there are functors
SKur →dOrb →Top.
The first arrow sends K = (G, V, E, S) to the quotient D = (V/G, E/G, S/G). Notice that a
conjugacy class of Kuranishi space embeddings induces a well-defined embedding of derived
orbifolds. The second arrow is induced by passing to the zero locus S−1(0) of a derived orbifold
D = (U, E, S).
4.1.1. Rigidified embeddings. We would like to introduce an analogue of rigidified embeddings for
orbifolds. We need to relax the triviality requirement. A flat orbifold vector bundle is an orbifold
vector bundle together with a flat connection. A key property of flat vector bundles is, if E →U
is flat and F →E is flat, then F is canonically a flat bundle over U.


## Page 33


INTEGRAL HAMILTONIAN FLOER THEORY
33
For example, if G is a compact Lie group acting on a smooth manifold V with only finite stabilizers
and W is a representation of G, then the vector bundle (V × W)/G is flat. Notice that the
pullback of rigid vector bundles is still rigid.
Definition 4.5. Let ϕ21 : D1 →D2 be an embedding of derived orbifolds.
(1) A rigidification of ϕ21 consists of a disk bundle N21 contained in a flat vector bundle
F21 →U1 and an open embedding
ψ21 : StabN21(D1) →D2
which extends ϕ21.
(2) Two rigidifications (N21 ⊆F21, ψ21), (N ′

(3) A rigidified embedding of derived orbifolds is an embedding together with an equivalence
class of rigidifications.
One can see that rigid derived orbifold embeddings can be composed. Therefore we can define a
category of derived orbifolds with rigid embeddings as morphisms. This category is denoted by
dOrbrig.
Notice that there is a quotient functor
SKurrig →dOrbrig.
4.2. Normal complex structures. Normal complex (NC for short) structures are essential for
constructing FOP transverse perturbations. We recall the definitions here.
4.2.1. Basic notions. Suppose G acts on a set A. A subgroup H ⊆G is called an A-essential
subgroup, denoted by
H ⊆A G
if there exists a ∈A whose stabilizer is H.
Given a real representation V of a finite group Γ, the basic decomposition of V is the splitting
V = VΓ ⊕ˇVΓ
where VΓ is the direct sum of trivial subrepresentations and ˇVΓ is the direct sum of nontrivial
irreducible subrepresentations. An NC structure on V is a Γ-invariant complex structure on ˇVΓ.
4.2.2. NC structures.
Definition 4.6. Let U be a Γ-manifold and E →U be a Γ-equivariant vector bundle. An
NC structure on E consists of, for each U-essential subgroup H ⊆U Γ, a H-invariant complex
structure I ˇ

ˇEH ⊆E|UH
satisfying the following conditions.
(1) For each g ∈Γ which conjugates H to H′ := gHg−1, the bundle isomorphism ˇEH →
ˇEgHg−1 sends I ˇ
EH to I ˇ

(2) For each pair of U-essential subgroups K ⊆H ⊆Γ, one has the K-equivariant decomposi-
tion
ˇEH ∼= ˇEK ⊕( ˇEH ∩EK|UH).
We require that the restriction of I ˇ

Definition 4.7. Let U be an effective orbifold and E →U be an orbifold vector bundle. An
NC structure on E consists of, for each bundle chart ˆC = (Γ, U, E) of E, an NC structure IE
on the vector bundle E, such that chart embeddings respect those complex structures. An NC
structure on U is by definition an NC structure on TU.
A normally complex derived orbifold is a derived orbifold D = (U, E, S) together with NC
structures on U and E.
Definition 4.8 (Embeddings of NC derived orbifolds).
(1) An open embedding of normally complex derived orbifolds from D1 to D2 is an
open embedding of derived orbifolds which respects normal complex structures.
(2) A stabilization of an NC derived orbifold is a stabilization by a disk bundle which is
contained in a complex orbifold vector bundle.
(3) An embedding of NC derived orbifolds ι21 : D1 →D2 is an embedding such that there
exists a stabilization of D1 by a disk bundle N21 →U1 and an open embedding from
StabN21(D1) →D2 which extends ι21.
(4) A rigidified embedding of NC derived orbifolds from D1 to D2 is a rigidified embedding
whose rigidification can be represented by a disk bundle in a rigid complex vector bundle.
Two rigidifications are equivalent if they (after appropriate shrinking to smaller disk
bundles) differ by an isomorphism of rigid complex vector bundles.
Therefore we obtained the category dOrbNC of normally complex derived orbifolds with morphisms
being embeddings and the category dOrbNC
rig with the same objects with morphisms being rigidified
embeddings. One can see that they are both regular stratification categories.
Lemma 4.9. There are natural functors of regular stratification categories
SKurNC →dOrbNC,
SKurNC
rig →dOrbNC
rig
Proof. Let K = (G, V, E, S) be a stably complex Kuranishi space. Then by definition, E has a
G-invariant complex structure and the vector bundle
TV/g
has a G-invariant stable complex structure. This means there exists a real vector space R and a
complex structure on
TV/g ⊕R.
Consider the quotient chart
K/G := (U, E, S)
where U = V/G. Given any x ∈V with stabilizer Gx ⊆G, one has the Gx-invariant decomposition
TxV ∼= TxVGx ⊕NxVGx.
The stable complex structure on TV/g induces a complex structure on NxVGx. The collection of
these complex structures forms a normal complex structure on the quotient orbifold. One can
easily check that the morphisms descend to morphisms in dOrbNC and dOrbNC
rig .
□

5. Collars
Various inductive constructions require moduli spaces, which appear as, e.g., morphism spaces of
flow categories, to have compatible collars. While one can possibly discuss collars in an abstract
manner, we refrain from doing so. Instead, in this section we only consider the categories with
geometric meanings. More precisely, in this section, the category C belongs to the list Top,
Man, SMan, ΨMan, Kur, dOrb etc. Following an idea of Fukaya, we explain how to equip the
geometric objects of interest with collars “on the outside” which facilitates inductive constructions
of various sorts.
5.1. The subcategory of collared objects. For the discussions in this section, let ϵ > 0 be a
positive real number. The model for collars is the space [0, ϵ)k which is stratified by subsets of
{1, . . . , k}. More generally, for any finite set S, we can form the collar [0, ϵ)S stratified by the set
A(S) (Notation 2.2). These standard collars are objects of all the relevant regular stratification
categories C listed above.
Definition 5.1. Let C be either Top, Man, ΨMan, SMan, Kur, SKur, or dOrb. For any
ϵ > 0, an ϵ-collaring of an A-stratified object M ∈ObC is a collection of open embeddings
∂αM × [0, ϵ)Faceγ
α →∂γM, α ≤γ
(called a collar embedding from α to γ) such that 1) its restriction to ∂αM is the inclusion of
∂αM into M and 2) whenever α ≤β ≤γ, the following diagram commutes.

in C is an object together with a germ-equivalence class of ϵ-collarings.

5.2. Outercollaring construction. For manifolds with boundary, it is well-known that one can
construct collars. However, it is not easy to find collars for more complicated objects, for example,
smooth manifolds with corners. Here we would like to adopt the outercollaring construction from
[FOOO20, Section 17] to avoid such discussions. Instead of finding collars in the given geometric
object, we add collars outside. In general, for each category C listed at the beginning of this
section, we would like to construct monoidal functors called the ϵ-outercollaring
{·} : C →⌊C.
Some care must be taken when we are in the smooth category.

where C is either SMan, or SKur, or dOrb. For each admissible object M, let the result be

manifolds are automatically normally complex orbifolds. Hence one has the collared versions

Example 6.2. A trivial flow category enriched in C, denoted by O, is the flow category whose

more general notion of flow multimodules allowing multiple flow categories as inputs, which model
situations such as the pair-of-pants product.

6.3. Lifts of flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies. The key issue of constructing
Floer chain complex (and various chain maps, homotopies) is to regularize certain moduli spaces.
Using the terminologies we just introduced, the associated flow categories (and multimodules,
homotopies), whose morphism spaces are certain moduli spaces, are enriched in Top.

6.5. Multimodule concatenations.
6.5.1. Bimodule concatenations. Before we discuss the more general multimodule concatenation,
we first consider the bimodule case, which was discussed in [AB24, Section 5].

Remark 6.14. We would like to reiterate that in Definition 6.13, X ◦i Y is part of the data: we do
not construct X ◦i Y using X and Y. This is in contrast with the gluing construction in [AB24,
Section 5], where the concatenation is carried out for bimodules enriched in dOrb by thickening
up the boundary strata. In our applications, all X ◦i Y arise from geometric context, and the
notations in Definition 6.13 already ensure the desired chain-level statements.

6.6.1. Multimodule concatenations as weak bimodule concatenations. Now we consider the situation
of multimodule concatenations.
Let X be a multimodule over (F1, . . . , Fm; Gi) and Y be a
multimodule over (G1, . . . , Gn; G′). The concatenation X ◦i Y at Gi is defined previously as a
multimodule over (G1, · · · , Gi−1, F1, . . . , Fm, Gi+1, . . . , Gn; G′) (together with other data).
As
multimodules can be treated as weak bimodules (Lemma 6.18), we can interpret the concatenation
as a concatenation of a pair of weak multimodules.
First, notice that the notion of concatenations can be extended from multimodules to weak
multimodules. Then we consider the product of weak bimodules
IG1G1 × · · · × IGi−1Gi−1 × X × IGi+1Gi+1 × · · · × IGnGn
as a weak bimodule over (G1 × · · · × Gi−1 × F1 × · · · × Fm × Gi+1 × · · · × Gn; G1 × · · · × Gn). Then
the multimodule concatenation X ◦i Y is equivalent to the weak bimodule concatenation
(IG1G1 × · · · × IGi−1Gi−1 × X × IGi+1Gi+1 × · · · × IGnGn) ◦Y.
The purpose of introducing this formal notion is to simplify the definition of equivariance of
multimodules when the symmetry group permutes the source flow categories.
7. Algebraic Structures
In this section we describe how to obtain algebraic structures such as chain complexes, chain
maps, and chain homotopies from, respectively, flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies.
When additional structures exist, these chain-level objects also carry corresponding structures
such as equivariance and module structures over Novikov rings.
7.1. Unobstructedness, local finiteness, grading, and orientations. We start by considering
the most basic properties of flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies that lead to chain-level
structures. Notice that these structures will only produce chain complex etc. as abelian groups.
To obtain objects as modules over various Novikov rings, one needs certain extra symmetries on
the flow categories etc.


## Page 51


INTEGRAL HAMILTONIAN FLOER THEORY
51
7.1.1. Unobstructedness. In geometric applications, the flow categories, multimodules, and homo-
topies always satisfy an additional property which does not have a general formulation. Here we
specify this property.
Definition 7.1. Let F be a flow category enriched in Top. It is said to be unobstructed12 if
for any triple p < r < q of objects, the structural morphism
ιF
prq : M F
pr × M F
rq →M F
pq
is a homeomorphism onto ∂prqM F
pq. Hence the union of these strata is the true boundary of M F
pq.
If C →Top is a functor of regular stratification categories and F is a flow category enriched in C,
F is called unobstructed if the induced flow category enriched in Top is unobstructed.
Similarly, we can define the notion of unobstructedness for multimodules and homotopies enriched
in Top. We omit the details. In applications within this paper, all geometric flow categories and
their lifts are unobstructed.
7.1.2. Convergence. We need certain properties guaranteeing the convergence of the chain-level
objects in certain nonarchimedean completions. To incorporate the equivariant case, we allow a
second functional which is bounded from one side to give a different completion.
Definition 7.2. A filtration on a (weak) flow category F is a monotonic function
A : ObF →Rk (k ≥1)
where Rk has the product partial order. The filtration is called locally finite if for each p ∈ObF
and any c > 0,

A (weak) flow category equipped with a locally finite filtration is called a locally finite (weak)
flow category.
Lemma 7.3. If (Fi, Ai), i = 1, . . . , m are locally finite filtered flow categories, then the product
(F1 × · · · × Fm, A1 ⊕· · · ⊕Am) is a locally finite weak flow category.
Proof. Straightforward.
□
Remark 7.4. In a typical Floer theoretic setup, the action A is R-valued. In the equivariant case
considered in Part 4 of this paper, the action will have two components.

7.1.3. Grading. Now we consider gradings on the flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies.
Let C be a regular stratification category which is either Kur, dOrb, or ΨMan. Define the
notion of virtual dimension by
dimvirC :=





dimU −dimG −rankE,
C = (G, U, E, S) ∈ObKur,
dimU −rankE,

C ∈ObΨMan
We allow different components of C to have different virtual dimensions.
Below we define gradings. In most of this paper, we will consider absolute, namely, Z-gradings; in
certain situations we will reduce to cyclic gradings.
Definition 7.10. Let N be a nonnegative integer. All flow categories and multimodules are
assumed to be enriched in either Kur, dOrb, or ΨMan.
(1) A (cohomological) Z-grading on a flow category F is a function p 7→|p| ∈Z satisfying
dimvirM F
pq = |q| −|p| −1, ∀p, q ∈ObF.
(2) Let F1, . . . , Fm, F′ be flow categories equipped with Z-gradings. A multimodule X over
(F1, . . . , Fm; F′) is said to have cohomological degree r if for all pi ∈ObFi and p′ ∈ObF′
dimvirM X
p1···pm;p′ = |p′| −|p1| −· · · −|pm| −r.
7.1.4. Orientations. Defining chain complexes in characteristics other than 2 requires orientations.
We do not include orientations as part of the structure on objects in the categories Kur, dOrb,
or ΨMan as it is cumbersome to discuss orientations on the corners. Instead, we regard it as an
extra structure on the flow category, multimodule, or homotopy.
Convention. Let M be an oriented manifold with boundary. The induced boundary orientation
is the one such that the
nout ∧or∂M ∈det TM
coincides (up to positive scalar) with the orientation orM of M.
Definition 7.11. Let C = (U, E, S) be an A-stratified derived orbifold with A being a normal
poset (Definition 2.5).
(1) For each element α ∈A, an orientation on ∂αC is an orientation of the virtual vector
bundle T∂αU −E|∂αV , or equivalently an orientation on the determinant line
det(∂αC) := det(T∂αU) ⊗det(E∗|T ∂αU).
Notice that for any face β ∈Faceα, an orientation on ∂αC induces an orientation on ∂βC
by regarding the collar coordinate to point “inward.”
(2) An orientation on C consists of orientations on all top strata ∂γC such that for each fake
boundary stratum β, the orientation on ∂βC induced from the (exactly) two adjacent top
strata are opposite.
(3) Let C′ = (U′, E′, S′) be another A′-stratified derived orbifold. An embedding ι : C →C′
is said to be orientation-preserving if the underlying poset map has degree zero (i.e.
maps top strata to top strata) and if for each top stratum γ ∈A (which is mapped to
γ′ ∈A′), the induced map of determinant lines
det(∂γC) →det(∂γ′C′)
is orientation-preserving.

7.3. Symmetries on flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies. In the geometric
situations considered in this paper, there are three types of symmetries on flow categories,
multimodules, and homotopies. The first is the typical kind of Novikov symmetry, which is
basically the symmetry related to changing cappings of contractible periodic orbits. The second
and the third only appear in the equivariant case. The second type is the symmetry on the Borel
model S∞∼= EZp, given by the monoid Z≥0, which shifts the coordinates, rather than the group
Z. The third is symmetry by a finite group Zp.
7.3.1. Twisted Novikov groups.
Definition 7.21 (Novikov groups).
(1) A Novikov group consists of a free abelian group Π together with morphisms
λ = (λ1, . . . , λk) : Π →Rk,
µ : Π →2Z

(2) The category of Novikov groups admits finite products, denoted by
(Π, λ, µ) ⊕(Π′, λ′, µ′) = (Π ⊕Π′, λ ⊕λ′, µ + µ′).
(3) An twisted Novikov group, denoted by Π ⋊G, consists of a Novikov group Π together
with a finite group G acting on Π by isomorphisms of Novikov groups. Notice that the
semi-direct product Π ⋊G is a not necessarily abelian.

(2) Z with the inclusion λ : Z ,→R and the multiplication by two µ : Z →2Z ,→Z is a
Novikov group.
(3) Any finite group is a twisted Novikov group.

7.4. Algebraic structures with symmetry. In the previous two subsections, we provide
separate treatments of convergence of structural maps of chain complexes and Novikov rings with
geometric origin. As usual, in the concrete setting of Floer theory, the two structures interact
with each other in the form that recapping of periodic orbits corresponds to multiplication by
Novikov variables. Abstractly, equivariance under Novikov groups of flow categories, multimodules,
and homotopies leads to linearity of differentials and chain maps under Novikov rings, and local
finiteness of orbits under the Novikov group action is compatible with the convergence assumption.
We record these classical observations here formally.
7.4.1. Flow categories and bimodules. Suppose (F, A) is a Z-graded, oriented, unobstructed,
Novikov flow category enriched in ΨMan, then we have obtained previously the complex C(F) as
a free abelian group.
Proposition 7.30. Let (Π ⋊G, λ, µ) be a twisted Novikov group. If (F, A) is Π⋊-equivariant,
then C(F) has the structure of a Z-graded ΛΠ
Z-module with a G-action and the differential is
G-equivariant and ΛΠ
Z-linear.
Proof. The Π-equivariance condition on A implies that C(F) has a natural ΛΠ
Z-module structure.
Moreover, the Π-equivariance on the flow category implies that d is a ΛΠ
Z-module map.

the presence of the finite group G, it follows from definition that the chain complex C(F) is
G-equivariant.

Remark 7.35. There is another notion called Floer–Novikov complex defined in [Ush08]. It
requires that the differential strictly decreases the nonarchimedean norm. On the other hand,
that notion allows one to consider Novikov rings with coefficients in a general Noetherian ring
and the exponents Γ to be a general finite-rank free abelian group. In the Floer setup, the Floer
complex can be made both a Floer–Novikov complex and a Floer-type complex with appropriate
Novikov coefficients.
Next we have two notions about chain maps and chain homotopies.
Definition 7.36. (cf. [UZ16, Definition 4.5]) Let C and D be two Floer-type complexes over ΛΠ
K.
(1) A linear map Φ : C →D is said to preserve the filtration if
ℓD(Φ(x)) ≤ℓC(x) ∀x ∈C.
(2) C and D are said to be filtered isomorphic if there is an isomorphism Φ : C →D of
chain complexes such that
ℓD(Φ(x)) = ℓC(x) ∀x ∈C.
(3) Two chain maps Φ, Ψ : C →D are said to be filtered chain homotopic if 1) both
Φ and Ψ preserve the filtration and 2) there exists a homotopy K : C →D between Φ
and Ψ which also preserves the filtration. Two Floer-type complexes are filtered chain
homotopy equivalent if there exists a filtered chain homotopy Φ : C →D and a filtered
homotopy inverse Ψ : D →C such that Φ ◦Ψ is filtered chain homotopic to IdD and
Ψ ◦Φ is filtered chain homotopic to IdC.
One uses the following notion to compare Floer type complexes.
Definition 7.37. Let (C, ℓC, dC) and (D, ℓD, dC) be two Floer-type complexes and δ ≥0. A δ-
quasiequivalence between C and D is a quadruple (Φ, Ψ, KC, KD) where Φ : C →D, Ψ : D →C
are chain maps, KC, KD are chain homotopies as
Ψ ◦Φ −IdC = dC ◦KC + KC ◦dC,
Φ ◦Ψ −IdD = dD ◦KD + KD ◦dD
satisfying for all x ∈C and y ∈D,
ℓD(Φ(x)) ≤ℓC(x) + δ,
ℓC(Ψ(y)) ≤ℓD(y) + δ,
and
ℓC(KC(x)) ≤ℓC(x) + 2δ,
ℓD(KD(y)) ≤ℓD(y) + 2δ.
The quasiequivalence distance between two Floer-type complexes is the infimum of δ for which
there exists a δ-quasiequivalence between them.
Below we show that the complex obtained from a Novikov flow category is indeed a Floer-type
complex, once we use field coefficients.

(1) The action λ on Π is R-valued and A is R-valued.
(2) ObF/Π is finite.
Then these data together with the differential on the chain complex C(F) define a filtered Floer–
Novikov complex in the sense of [Ush08, Definition 1.1].

7.5.3. Barcodes. By the nonarchimedean Gram–Schmidt process established in [UZ16, Definition
6.3], given a Floer-type complex (C, ℓC, dC) = (C, ℓ, d), the map d : C →C of the ungraded
complex factors through C →ker(d) ⊆C, so we can find an orthogonal basis y1, . . . , yn of C
and an orthogonal basis of x1, . . . , xm of ker(d) such that one can write d : C →ker(d) in the

The verbose barcode of (C, d, ℓ) is the multiset of elements (R/Γ) × [0, +∞] consisting of
(1) a pair (ℓ(xi) mod Γ, ℓ(yi) −ℓ(xi)) for i = 1, . . . , r.
(2) a pair (ℓ(xi) mod Γ, ∞) for i = r + 1, . . . , m.
Each element of this multiset is called a bar while the second variable is called the bar-length.
The concise barcode is the submultiset obtained by removing bars of length zero. It was proved
([UZ16, Theorem 7.1]) that the verbose barcode of a Floer-type complex does not depend on the
choice of the singular value decomposition, hence is well-defined.

8. FOP Perturbations
In this section we review the technique of FOP perturbations. The original idea was proposed
by Fukaya–Ono in [FO97], and further explored by Parker [Par13]. In [BX22b] the authors
rigorously established this technique and defined integer-valued Gromov–Witten invariants for
general compact symplectic manifolds. In [BX22a] the authors further proved the integral Arnold
conjecture; see [BPX24] for another application.
8.1. FOP perturbations.
Definition 8.1 (Perturbations on derived orbifolds).
Given a derived orbifold D = (U, E, S), a
perturbation of D (or of S) is a smooth section S′ : U →E such that if S−1(0) is compact, then
(S′)−1(0) is also compact.
We recall the main “black-box” type theorem proved in [BX22b].
Theorem 8.2. [BX22b, Theorem 1.1] Suppose U is an NC orbifold without boundary14 and E →U
is an NC vector bundle. Let Γ(U, E) be the space of smooth sections. Then there is a C0-dense
subset ΓFOP(U, E) ⊆Γ(U, E) whose elements are called FOP transverse sections satisfying the
following properties.
(1) (Classical transversality) If U is a manifold, FOP transversality is equivalent to
classical transversality.
(2) (Locality Property) The restrictions of FOP transverse sections to open subsets are
still FOP transverse.
(3) (Extension Property) For any pair of closed subsets Y ⊆Y′ and open neighborhoods
V ⊆U of Y and V′ ⊆U of Y′, if S ∈ΓFOP(V, E|V), then there exists S′ ∈ΓFOP(V′, E|V′)
which agrees with S near Y.
(4) (Product Property) Let U′ be another normally complex orbifold and E′ →U′ be a
normally complex vector bundle. Then the product map
Γ(U, E) × Γ(U′, E′) →Γ(U × U′, E ⊞E′)
sends products of FOP transverse sections to FOP transverse sections.
(5) (Stabilization Property) If πF : F →U is rigid complex vector bundle15, then the
stabilization map
Γ(U, E) →Γ(F, π∗
FE ⊕π∗
FF),
S 7→π∗
FS ⊕τF,
where τF : F →π∗
FF is the tautological section, sends FOP transverse sections to FOP
transverse sections.
(6) (Main stratum) For each S ∈ΓFOP(U, E), the closure
S−1(0) ∩Ufree
is a pseudomanifold (whose dimension is dimU −rankE).

8.2. FOP perturbations on flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies. Now we
prove a major result of this paper stating the general procedure of constructing FOP perturbations
on flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies.
Theorem N. Flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies in this proposition are assumed to

assume that all charts have compact zero locus.

rig . Then there exists a Π ⋊G-equivariant FOP perturbation ˚F on ˜F.

Part 2. GEOMETRIC FLOW CATEGORIES, BIMODULES, AND HOMOTOPIES
Outline of Part 2
In this part, we describe flow categories, bimodules, and homotopies that will appear in (most of)
the settings of Hamiltonian Floer theory. We then state the main theorems about the construction
of their lifts to the category of normally complex Kuranishi spaces, which induce lifts to normally
complex derived orbifolds. Using the the counting scheme described in Section 8, we prove the
main theorems except for those in the Zp-equivariant setting. The technical constructions of the
regularization of the moduli spaces are deferred to the next Part.
9. Geometric Flow Categories
9.1. Morse flow category. Given a closed oriented Riemannian manifold (M, g) and a Morse
function f : M →R, the Morse flow category, denoted by
M(f, g)
and usually abbreviated as M, has critical points of f as its objects and the moduli spaces of
unparametrized (broken) downward gradient trajectories as its morphism spaces. We always
assume that (f, g) is Morse–Smale and that near critical points f is a quadratic function and g
is the Euclidean metric with respect to a certain coordinate system near each critical point. In
this way, for any two critical points p, q ∈critf, the moduli space MM
pq is a smooth manifold with
corners [Weh12].
The partial order of ObM is defined as
p ≤q ⇐⇒MM
pq ̸= ∅
and the poset stratifying MM
pq (when p ̸= q)
AM
pq = {p < r1 < · · · < rk < q}.
Then MM
pq is an object of SMan.
We also define composition maps as the natural inclusion
MM
pr × MM
rq →MM
pq
which is a morphism of SMan. Therefore, we obtained a flow category enriched in SMan. In
fact, the standard exponential decay estimate implies that the moduli spaces are indeed objects
of SManadm. Therefore, its outercollaring still lives in the same category.
The flow category M(f, g) admits a grading which assigns to each critical point p the its Morse index.
By assigning orientations on unstable manifolds and counting elements of zero-dimensional moduli
spaces, one obtains a Morse–Smale–Witten complex CM∗(f, g) over integers freely generated by
critical points, whose homology is isomorphic to the singular homology of M.
9.2. Floer flow category. We now describe a central object considered in this paper. We will
assign to each compact symplectic manifold together with a nondegenerate Hamiltonian and an
almost complex structure a flow category, called the Floer flow category, typically denoted by
F. More precisely, let (X, ω) be a compact symplectic manifold. Recall
Π =
π2(X)
kerc1 ∩kerω

Situation Flow(i). (X, ω) is a compact symplectic manifold.
F is the Floer flow category
associated to a nondegenerate 1-periodic Hamiltonian and a 1-periodic family of ω-compatible

We summarize the usual properties of the Floer flow category.
Proposition 9.1. In Situation Flow(i), the Floer flow category F, together with the symplectic
action AH and the Conley–Zehnder index, is a Z-graded Π-equivariant Novikov flow category.

9.3. The pearly flow category. To compare Floer homology and Morse homology, we introduce
a version of the pearly flow category. A priori, this flow category induces a chain complex which
is not quasi-isomorphic to the Morse chain complex, as least not in the most naive way.
Let (X, ω, J) be a compact almost K¨ahler manifold. For each a ∈Π, consider the moduli space
MR
0,2(a)
which is a variant of the moduli space M0,2(a) of J-holomorphic maps with two marked points
in degree d. It is defined to be the moduli space of unparametrized Floer trajectories for the
Hamiltonian H ≡0. By singularity removal, MR
0,2(a) can be alternatively described as the moduli
space of J-holomorphic spheres of class a with 2 marked points such that the 2 marked points are
equipped with asymptotic markers with the same angular coordinate.
There is a natural compactification
MR
0,2(a)
which is naturally stratified by (ordered) decompositions a = a1 + · · · + ak with ω(ai) > 0. The
strata of the above moduli space MR
0,2(a) can be labelled by the so-called linear trees. These
are oriented trees whose vertices have valence at most 2, hence have a unique incoming vertex
(the input) and a unique outgoing vertex (the output). The degrees a1, . . . , ak ∈Π are viewed
as extra decorations on the vertices. We then can allow the edges of the tree to acquire length,
allowing the lengths to vary, and allowing the edges to break when the lengths approach infinity.
Choose a Morse–Smale pair (f, g) on X. We then add additional variables which are negative
gradient segments of (f, g) defined on the edges of varying length. For the input sphere resp.
output sphere we also add a semi-infinite edge on which is supported a negative gradient ray.
The natural matching conditions at nodes are required. By choosing an incoming resp. outgoing
critical point x resp. y of f, one can consider the pearly moduli space and its compactification
MP
xy(a).
The underlying poset is not as simple as the Morse or Floer case. For x = (x, a) and y = (x, b) in
critf × Π, define AP
xy to be the set of (possibly broken) linear trees whose breakings are labelled
by critical points of f, whose vertices are labelled by elements of Π (with sum being a −b), and
whose finite edges have either zero or positive lengths. This is a regular poset, whose maximal
elements are those unbroken trees whose edges all have positive length. The depth function is
given by the number of breakings plus the number of edges with length zero.
Definition 9.2. The pearly flow category P associated to X, J, f, g as above, is the flow
category enriched in Top which is defined as follows.
(1) The set of objects is ObP = critf × Π.
(2) For any pair of objects x = (x, a) and y = (y, b), the morphism space is the moduli space
MP
xy(a −b).
(3) The composition map is given by the concatenation of pearly solutions.
Remark 9.3. The pearly flow category P should be distinguished from the pearly bimodule
introduced in [BX22a, Section 4.4], which is a flow bimodule between Morse flow categories. P
here is used to set up the Morse–Bott homology of the constant Hamiltonian H ≡0.
9.4. Coupling of geometric flow categories. Given symplectic manifolds (Xj, ωj), j = 1, . . . , k
and Floer flow categories Fj associated to pairs (Hj, Jj), their coupling is defined to be the
Floer flow category of the product manifold with the product Hamiltonians and almost complex
structure. However, it is difficult to describe this construction on the abstract level for a collection

SHAOYUN BAI AND GUANGBO XU
of flow categories. The more abstract packaging would require building a symmetric monoidal
structure on the (infinity) category of flow categories, cf. [AB24] and its follow-up work. Here we
describe certain “coupling” that arises geometrically.
Running Assumption. Let Fj, j = 1, . . . , k be a collection of flow categories. Each of them is
either a Floer, Morse, or pearly flow category on a compact (symplectic) manifold.
We define their coupling, denoted as
F := F1 ∗· · · ∗Fk,
as follows. Its set of objects is
ObF = ObF1 × · · · × ObFk
whose elements are written as p = (p1, . . . , pk). The morphism space Mpq is the moduli space of
solutions u = (u1, . . . , uk) where uj is a (stable) Floer/Morse/pearly trajectory connecting pj and
qj, depending on the geometric origin of Fj. We take the quotient by the “synchronized” R-action,
i.e., for any s ∈R, we identify tuples of parametrized trajectories
(u1(·), . . . , uk(·)) ∼(u1(· + s), . . . , uk(· + s)),
and compactify by allowing sphere bubbling and breaking of trajectories.
Notice that this
formulation means that breakings in each factor happen simultaneously. For example, when all
Fj are Floer flow categories, this coupling is the same as the Floer flow category of the product
manifold with respect to the product Hamiltonian and product almost complex structure. Notice
that if Fj is equivariant with respect to an abelian group Πj, then F is equivariant with respect
to the natural action by the product Π = Π1 × · · · × Πk.
In the discussion of equivariant Floer theory, one often needs to take self-coupling. For any k,
denote
F∗k := F ∗· · · ∗F
|

}

which is equivariant with respect to the action by the product Π × · · · × Π of k copies of Π. On
the other hand, Zk acts on this flow category by cyclically permuting the factors. This Zk-action
commutes with the action by the diagonal Π. Hence this coupled flow category is equivariant
with respect to an action by Π × Zk.
10. Geometric Multimodules
Next, we describe bimodules and multimodules that lead to continuation maps, PSS and SSP
maps, pair-of-pants products, and K¨unneth maps.
10.1. Floer data and Floer domains.
Definition 10.1. A punctured surface is a compact oriented surface (not necessarily connected)
with finitely many points removed, usually denoted by Σ. We also include as part of data a
decomposition of the punctures into negative ends and positive ends. The negative ends are
usually denoted by z1
−, . . . , zk−
−, and the positive ends are usually denoted by z1
+, . . . , zk+
+ .
A cylindrical end on a punctured surface Σ around a negative resp. positive puncture zj
± is an
orientation-preserving diffeomorphism between R± × S1 and a punctured neighborhood of zj
±; two
cylindrical ends are regarded as equivalent if they differ by a translation. An equivalence class is
called a germ of cylindrical ends.
A cylindrical surface is a punctured surface equipped with germs of cylindrical ends around all
punctures. To save notations such an object is still denoted by Σ.

Situation Module(i). (X, ω) is a compact symplectic manifold. F1, . . . , Fm; F′ 16 are Floer
flow categories associated to pairs (H1, J1), . . . , (Hm, Jm); (H′, J′). X is the multimodule over
(F1, . . . , Fm; F) associated to a smooth Floer domain ΣX with m negative cylindrical ends and one
positive cylindrical end. Moreover, the Floer datum on ΣX coincides with (Hjdt, Jj) on the j-th
negative end and (H′dt, J′) on the positive end.
Proposition 10.3. In Situation Module(i), the multimodule X is a Π-equivariant Novikov
multimodule over (F1, . . . , Fm; F′).
We give examples of a few concrete cases which appear naturally in applications.
10.2.1. Bimodules for continuation maps. The first one is the bimodule coming from the contin-
uation map construction. Given Floer flow categories F± associated to a pair (H±, J±) on the
same symplectic manifold (X, ω), a homotopy of the Floer data
(j0, Hsdt, Js,t),

s→±∞Hs = H±, lim
s→± Js,t = J±
over the infinite cylinder, where j0 is the standard complex structure on R × S1, is a special case
of Floer data over an arbitrary punctured sphere. Hence one obtains an (F−, F+)-module, usually
called the bimodule of continuation maps.
There is a special one corresponding to the constant Floer datum on the infinite cylinder.
Definition 10.4. The diagonal bimodule of a Floer flow category F associated to (H, J) is the
bimodule for continuation maps associated to the s-independent Floer data (j0, Hdt, J) on the
infinite cylinder. It is denoted by ∆FF.
10.2.2. Poincar´e duality multimodule. Consider a surface ΣPD with two negative cylindrical ends
and no positive ends. Let σPD be a Floer datum on ΣPD which converges to H1dt and H2dt on
the two negative ends. Let F1 and F2 be the Hamiltonian Floer flow categories associated to H1
and H2. On the other hand, consider the trivial flow category O whose objects are elements of
Π and the only morphisms are identities. Then we describe a multimodule XPD over (F1, F2; O).

a ∈Π. We compactify the space by allowing sphere bubbles as well as Floer breakings at the two

10.4. Concatenations. We describe the situation corresponding to concatenating Floer data on
different domains.
Situation Concatenation(i). (X, ω) is a compact symplectic manifold. F1, . . . , Fm, G1, . . . , Gn, G′
are Floer flow categories associated to pairs (H1, J1), . . . , (Hm, Jm), (G1, I1), . . . , (Gn, In), (G′, I′)
respectively. X resp. Y is a multimodule over (F1, . . . , Fm; Gi) resp. over (G1, . . . , Gn; G′) associated
to smooth Floer domain ΣX resp. ΣY as described in Situation Module(i).
Proposition 10.5. In Situation Concatenation(i), the concatenation X ◦i Y is a Π-equivariant
Novikov multimodule over (G1, . . . , Gi−1, F1, . . . , Fm, Gi+1, . . . , Gn; G′).
Proof. Straightforward.

11. Homotopies of Multimodules
In this section we descibe several geometrically defined bimodule homotopies. As we only use
bimodule homotopies to show certain chain maps are canonically defined up to homotopy, we will
not provide the most general treatment. Hence, we only give a minimal descriptions of bimodules
which will be used in this paper.

Remark 11.2. The homotopy HFP˜◦PF,2 is used to relate two different constructions of the Morse–
Bott theory for the constant Hamiltonian H = 0. The pearl flow category includes choosing
a Morse function over the critical submanifolds and corresponds to the “cascades model” (see,
e.g., [Bou02]) of Morse–Bott theory. In contrast, the natural output of the bimodule HFP˜◦PF,1
corresponds to the “fiber product model” (see [AB95] for an early incarnation). The point of our
construction is to compare the “Morse version” of Floer theory, coming from the flow category F
that is associated with a nondegenerate Hamiltonian, with the Morse–Bott version, which uses
pearly trajectories, using the fiber product model as an intermediate gadget.
