11.3.2. Other concatenations along Pearly flow categories. We can also construct a homotopy
from BMF to the concatenation BMP ◦BPF, denoted by HMP˜◦PF and a homotopy from BFM to the
concatenation BFP ◦BPM, denoted by HFP˜◦PM. Since the two cases are dual to each other, we only
explain the definition of the first homotopy.
Consider the bimodule BMF given by the collection of moduli spaces
M MF
xp
which is the natural compactification of the space of solutions to the Floer equation on the infinite
cylinder with a Hamiltonian connection σ which is zero near −∞and which is Hdt near +∞. We
deform σ to a 1-parameter family στ, τ ∈[0, 1] such that when τ →1, the support of στ is pushed
to +∞. Then as τ = 1, one obtains a bimodule consisting of moduli spaces whose configurations
are similar to the third picture of Figure 6. Then one can continue the homotopy by inserting
gradient segments of lengths which vary from 0 to +∞. The very end of this homotopy is the
concatenation BMP ◦BPF.
11.3.3. Remarks on concatenation at the Morse flow category. One can also form the concatenation
at the Morse flow category, for example, BFM ◦BMF. In the category Top, it is homotopic to the
bimodule ∆FF, firstly by gluing broken Morse trajectories and secondly by gluing holomorphic
curves. However, it is hard to regularize such a homotopy. In particular, when gluing holomorphic
curves at a node, unlike the Pearly case, the gluing has a complex gluing parameter. This does
not fit very well with our framework. This is the reason why we need to go through the Pearly
flow category in order to prove the invertibility of the PSS and SSP maps.
12. Main Theorems on AMS Lifts
In this section, we state the main results regarding regularizations of moduli spaces introduced in
the previous section. The proof will be presented in the next part.
12.1. Main statements for Floer flow categories. We state the main theorem about global
charts of Floer flow categories and other types of flow categories. The constructions depend
on many choices. Most of the dependence of the choices can be absorbed via the global chart
construction of bimodules; however, the dependence of the outercollaring width and the choice of
the so-called integral action (Definition 15.1) cannot be absorbed easily. Hence in the statement,
we emphasize the dependence of these two choices.
Theorem O. In Situation Flow(i), given any outercollaring width ϵ > 0 and a choice of integral
action on F (Definition 15.1) on F, the ϵ-outercollaring of F admits a set of lifts ˜F to ⌊dOrbNC
rig
(Definition 3.12) as a Π-equivariant Novikov flow category. Each individual lift in this set is called
an AMS lift subject to the outercollaring width and the integral action. The AMS lifts admit
coherent orientations in the sense of Definition 7.12. Moreover, for any ϵ′ > 0, the ϵ′-outercollaring
of ˜F is an AMS lift of F subject to the outercollaring width ϵ + ϵ′ and the same integral action.
Proof. See Section 18 except for the last statement, which is a straightforward consequence of the
constructions.
□
Remark 12.1. In fact what we mean by an AMS lift does not only mean the resulting flow category
˜F, but also all the choices made in the process of construction. When an follow-up construction
depends on a given AMS lift, it means the construction a priori depends on the choices made for
the AMS lift.

Proposition 12.2. In Situation Flow(i), let ˜F1 and ˜F2 be two AMS lifts of F subject to the same
outercollaring width and two different integral actions, provided by Theorem O. Then there exists
a class of lifts of the corresponding outercollaring of ∆FF to ⌊dOrbNC
rig as a bimodule over (˜F1; ˜F2).
Moreover, with respect to the coherent orientations on ˜F1, ˜F2, the lift is coherently oriented.
Proof. See Section 20.

The (in)dependence on other choices for the AMS lift is included in the theorem on multimodules
(Theorem P).

Definition 15.2) in the construction.

13. Proof of the Main Theorems
In this section we prove (most of) the main theorems using previously stated theorems on Kuranishi
lifts of various flow categories, bimodules, homotopies and the theorem on FOP perturbations
(Theorem N). The proofs are mostly formal consequences of the existence of the regularizations
and coherent FOP perturbations thereon, following the general theme of Floer theory which turns
bordifications of moduli spaces into algebraic structures.
13.1. Proof of Theorem A. Suppose we are in Situation Flow(i).
Theorem O provides,
depending on a choice, an AMS lift ˜F enriched in ⌊dOrbNC
rig together with a coherent orientation.
As the Floer flow category is an unobstructed (Definition 7.1) and Π-equivariant Novikov flow
category (Definition 7.2 and Definition 7.24), Theorem N provides a Π-equivariant lift, i.e., an
FOP perturbation ˚F on ˜F, which is a flow category enriched in ⌊dOrbFOP
rig . Then apply the
forgetful functor (i.e., taking the closure of the isotropy-free locus of the Kuranishi section)
⌊dOrbFOP
rig
→ΨMan,
one obtains a flow category enriched in ΨMan. Using the coherent orientation and the Z-grading
by the Conley–Zehnder index, by Proposition 7.16 and Proposition 7.30, one obtains a canonically
associated chain complex
CF∗(H, J; Ξ) := C∗(˚F)
generated by objects of F, which has the structure of a graded ΛΠ
Z-module. Here Ξ denotes all
choices made in constructing the AMS lift and the FOP perturbation; in particular, Ξ includes
the information of the outercollaring width and the integral action. We call this process the basic
construction for Floer complex.


## Page 95


INTEGRAL HAMILTONIAN FLOER THEORY
95
We prove that the Floer chain complex has a well-defined chain homotopy type. Let Ξ(0), Ξ(1) be
two different sets of choices, including outercollaring widths, integral actions, and choices made
for the two AMS lifts ˜F(0), ˜F(1) and the FOP perturbations ˚F(0), ˚F(1).
Lemma 13.1. There exists a chain homotopy equivalence
Ψ(01) : CF∗(H, J, Ξ(0)) →CF∗(H, J, Ξ(1))
whose chain homotopy type is well-defined.
Proof. By the part of Theorem O about outercollaring width, one can reduce the comparison to
the case that the outercollaring widths are equal. Then by Proposition 12.2, there exists a lift of
the outercollaring of the diagonal bimodule ∆FF to ⌊dOrbNC
rig as a bimodule over (˜F(0); ˜F(1)). Then
by Theorem N, the FOP perturbations ˚F(0) and ˚F(1) can be extended to an FOP perturbation
˚∆FF on ˜∆FF. Then by Proposition 7.31, one obtains the chain map
Ψ(01) : CF∗(H, J, Ξ(0)) →CF∗(H, J, Ξ(1)).
Using the part of Proposition 12.2 stating that two lifts of ∆FF are homotopic and Theorem N,
one can prove that the homotopy type of Ψ(01) is well-defined.
It remains to prove that Ψ(01) is a homotopy equivalence. Indeed, for each object p ∈ObF, the
bimodule moduli space M FF
p;p consists of a single point corresponding to the constant trajectory at
p. This moduli space has no lower strata, hence coincides with its outercollaring. Moreover, it
is transversely cut out, so the FOP perturbation can be chosen to be the same as the original
Kuranishi section. We can then choose the FOP perturbation ˚∆FF inductively such that we do
not perturb for M FF
p;p. For such a choice of FOP perturbation, the count nFF
p;p needed to define
the corresponding coefficient of the chain map is 1. With respect to the natural identification
of the chain groups CF∗(H, J, Ξ(0)) ∼= CF∗(H, J, Ξ(1)), Ψ(01) is a higher order perturbation of
the identity in terms of T-valuation. Therefore, Ψ(01) is an invertible chain map, hence a chain
homotopy equivalence.
□
This finishes the proof of Theorem A.

Lemma 13.4. In Situation Homotopy(i), fix an outercollaring width and a compatible set of
integral actions on involved Floer flow categories. Let
CF∗(Hj, Jj, Ξj),

over (˚F1, . . . ,˚Fm;˚F′). Then by Theorem Q, there exists an AMS lift ˜H as a homotopy from
˜X0 to ˜X1. Then by Theorem N, the existing FOP perturbations can be extended to an FOP
perturbation ˚H on ˜H. Then Proposition 7.32 implies that the chain maps are homotopic via a
concrete homotopy from ˚H.

Proposition 13.6. Suppose F0, F1, F2 are Floer flow categories associated to three pairs (Hi, Ji)

such that the last one is homotopic to the composition ΦB12 ◦ΦB01.

13.3.5. Continuation maps are homotopy equivalences.
Corollary 13.7. Any chain-level continuation map is a homotopy equivalence.
Proof. Using the notations in Proposition 13.6, take F2 = F0 and denote B12 by B10. Let B02
be the diagonal bimodule ∆F0F0 associated to the trivial Floer datum on the cylinder. Choose a
homotopy H from B01 ◦B10 to ∆F0F0 by gluing Floer data and varying in a one-parameter family,
Proposition 13.6 provides a chain homotopy between the composition Φ01 ◦Φ10 and the chain
map associated to an AMS lift of ∆F0F0. Notice that the latter has the form
Φ∆FF = Id + higher order in T
hence is an invertible chain map. Therefore, by a similar argument switching the roles of Φ01 and
Φ10, the continuation maps Φ01 and Φ10 are homotopy inverses to each other.
□

13.4. Proof of Theorem C.
13.4.1. Definition of the pair-of-pants products. The setting of Theorem C falls into Situation
Module(i). Let ΣX be a smooth pair-of-pants with two negative ends and one positive end equipped
with a Floer datum which are asymptotic to H1dt and H2dt on the negative ends and to H′dt
on the positive end. To simplify the argument, use a fixed almost complex structure J; more
general cases can be treated with the help of continuation maps. Hence we omit the reference to
almost complex structures. Let F1, F2, F′ be the Floer flow categories associated to H1, H2, and
H′ respectively.
We fix an outercollaring width and will not mention it anymore in this subsection. Let X be
the multimodule associated to ΣX. Then by Proposition 15.4, one can choose a triple of integral
actions AΩ
F1, AΩ
F2, AΩ
F′. Then one obtains Floer chain complexes
CF∗(H1, Ξ1), CF∗(H2, Ξ2), CF∗(H′, Ξ′)
which depend on the AMS lifts ˜F1, ˜F2, ˜F′ subject to the integral actions and their FOP perturbations
˚F1,˚F2,˚F′. Upon choosing a pair of lateral lines in ΣX, the basic construction provides a chain map
ΦX(Θ) : CF∗(H1, Ξ1) ⊗CF∗(H2, Ξ2) →CF∗(H′, Ξ′)
which depends on an AMS lift ˜X and an FOP perturbation ˚X.
13.4.2. Well-defined homotopy class. Now we prove Part (1) of Theorem C. Similar to the case of
continuation maps, by Lemma 13.2, the homotopy class of ΦX(Θ) does not depend on choices
made for constructing the AMS lift ˜X and FOP perturbation ˚X, so a priori only depends on the
integral actions and the choices of lateral lines. These dependence is removed (up to homotopy)
by Lemma 13.3. Hence the homotopy class of ΦX(Θ) a priori only depends on the Floer data
defining X. Lastly, if X0 and X1 are multimodules defined by two different Floer data on the
pair-of-pants (including different cylindrical ends), then one can build a homotopy H from X0
to X1 by varying the Floer data. Then Lemma 13.4 implies that the corresponding chain maps
ΦX0(Θ0) and ΦX1(Θ1) are also homotopic.
13.4.3. Compatibility with the continuation maps. We only treat the case with composing continu-
ation maps at the positive end, and the other cases follow from the same reasoning. Let F′′ be a
fourth Floer flow category associated to (H′′, J′′). We assume that the almost complex structure
is fixed and drop it from the notation; the general case when J is domain-dependent can be
treated using continuation maps. Let BF′F′′ be a bimodule over (F′; F′′) provided by continuation
map Floer data on the cylinder. Consider the concatenation X ◦BF′F′′. Then by gluing the Floer
domains, one obtains a homotopy H from X ◦BF′F′′ to a multimodule Y over (F1, F2; F′′) defined
on the pair-of-pants with another Floer datum.
Then by Proposition 15.4, one can choose integral actions on involved Floer flow categories which
are compatible with respect to X, BF′F′′, Y, and H. Then we are in the situation of Lemma 13.5,
which provides specific chain complexes
CF∗(H1, Ξ1), CF∗(H2, Ξ2), CF∗(H′, Ξ′), CF∗(H′′, Ξ′′)

13.5. Proof of Theorem D. We establish the Floer-theoretic Poincar´e duality. Consider the
punctured Riemann sphere ΣPD which has two negative ends and no positive end. A Floer datum
whose restrictions to the two negative ends are (Hidt, Ji), i = 1, 2, leads to the multimodule XP D
over (F1, F2; ˜O) where ˜O is the Π-covering of the trivial flow category O. In particular, Ob˜O ∼= Π.
Now we define hte Poincar´e pairing on the chain level. By Proposition 15.4, one can choose
integral actions on F1, F2 compatible with respect to XPD. Fix an outercollaring width. By

Theorem A, one obtains chain complexes

ΦPD : CF∗(H1, J1, Ξ1) ⊗CF∗(H2, J2, Ξ2) →C∗(˜O) ∼= ΛΠ.
In the same way as proving independence of choices, one can see that ΦPD has a well-defined
homotopy type which does not depend on choices made for the AMS construction and FOP
perturbations. It is also compatible with continuation maps, and compatible with the comparison
between different choices of integral actions (here we do not need lateral lines). Hence the Poincar´e
pairing is well-defined.
We need to prove the compatibility between the Poincar´e pairing and the pair-of-pants product.
In fact this is similar to the proof of the associativity of the pair-of-pants product, using the con-
catenation of a multimodule X (for the pair-of-pants products) and XPD and a gluing construction,
utilizing Lemma 13.4 and Lemma 13.5. We omit the details.
Lastly, we prove the nondegeneracy of the Poincar´e pairing with field coefficients. We only need
to prove it on the homology level. We make use of the PSS map ΦMF regarded as a map on
homology. One can see that the composition
ΦPD ◦(ΦMF ⊗ΦMF)
is a bilinear pairing on H∗(M; ΛΠ
K). The pairing has a leading order term which can be identified
with the classical Poincar´e pairing on the integral homology of X. Hence the induced pairing is still
nondegenerate on the Morse homology (tensoring with ΛΠ
K). As the PSS map is an isomorphism
on homology (Theorem F), it implies that the Poincar´e pairing on the Floer homology is also
nondegenerate. This finishes the proof of Theorem D.
13.6. Proof of Theorem E. The definition of the PSS and the SSP maps are simply special

BFM; a backward interpolation provides a Morse-to-Floer bimodule BMF.

one can find AMS lifts ˜BMF and ˜BFM (which are enriched in ⌊dOrbNC
rig ) of BMF and BFM. Notice
that ⌊M can be viewed as enriched in ⌊dOrbFOP

by Theorem N, to an FOP perturbation ˚BMF and an FOP perturbation ˚BFM. Then one obtains
chain maps

ΦFM : CF∗(H, J, Ξ) →CM∗(f, g).
We call them the chain-level PSS map and the chain-level SSP map.

realized by gluing Floer cylinders, one can show that the PSS and SSP maps are compatible with

Part 3. THE AMS CONSTRUCTION
Outline of Part 3
We go through the AMS construction of lifts of various geometric flow categories, multimodules,
and homotopies to the category of (collared rigidified) smooth normally complex Kuranishi spaces.
14. The Flow Categories, Multimodules, and Homotopies of Domains
The Abouzaid–McLean–Smith (AMS) construction of global charts for genus zero Gromov–Witten
theory [AMS21] uses certain auxiliary moduli spaces of rational curves in projective spaces. To
extend such construction to the case of Floer moduli spaces, we consider an analogy, which is an
auxiliary flow category. The final goal of this section is to describe a flow category enriched in an
intermediate category. The discussion in this section and subsequent ones has certain overlap
with [BX22a, Section 5], but we include these expositions to make the paper self-contained and
various technical aspects more streamlined.
14.1. Monotone flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies.
14.1.1. Monotone flow category. We introduce the technical notion of monotone flow categories
to simplify the discussions.
We introduce the following normal poset used frequently in this paper. For each d ≥0, define

14.2. The category of curves. The following definition may not be the most canonical one.
However, as we will consider only very concrete objects, the ad hoc description suffices our purpose.
We should remind the reader that the following definition goes beyond the algebro-geometric
situation as the base B is typically only a stratified smooth manifold.
Definition 14.6. An equivariant families of curves is a triple C = (G, B, C) where G is a
(not necessarily reductive) complex Lie group containing a maximal compact subgroup G ⊆G, B
and C are stratified smooth G-manifolds, with a G-equivariant map C →B, and the structure
of a prestable genus zero complex curve on each fiber Cϕ ⊆C. This triple needs to satisfy the
following conditions.
(1) For any g ∈G and ϕ ∈B, the map g : Cϕ →Cgϕ is an isomorphism of complex curves.
(2) Let ˚
C ⊆C be the complement of fiberwise nodes. Then ˚
C is a stratified G-manifold and
the map ˚
C →B is a submersion.
For each object C = (G, B, C), we introduce
PC = G/G,
QC = Lie(G)/LieG.
We can also add additional structures to an equivariant family of curves, such as a collection of
marked points, which are precisely G-equivariant sections of C →B avoiding nodes and each
other. Another structure we will use all the time is the cylindrical ends around markings and
nodes, which are choices of germs of cylindrical coordinates.
We define morphisms of the category of equivariant families of curves.
Definition 14.7. Let C = (G, B, C) be an equivariant family of curves.
(1) A group enlargement of C = (G, B, C) is an equivariant family of curves C′ = (G′, B′, C′)
where G →G′ is a complex Lie group embedding which sends the compact group G into
G′ with B′ = G′ ×G B and C′ = G′ ×G C. We denote the group enlargement as such by
G′ ×G C.
(2) A strict morphism from C1 = (G1, B1, C1) to C2 = (G2, B2, C2), denoted by ζ21 : C1 →C2,
consists of a complex Lie group embedding ζG

SHAOYUN BAI AND GUANGBO XU
(3) A strict morphism is called a strict embedding if the induced map G2 ×G1 C1 →C2 is
an isomorphism.
(4) A unitary conjugation of (G, B, C) is a strict morphism induced from an element g ∈G
which lifts the conjugation action of g on G.
(5) An embedding of equivariant families of curves is a unitary conjugacy class of strict
embeddings, where we declare that two strict embeddings C1 →C2 are equivalent if one
can be taken to another via a unitary conjugation of g ∈G2.
(6) The category of equivariant families of curves, denoted by Curve, is the category whose
objects are equivariant families of curves and whose morphisms are embeddings.
Notice that the category Curve is monoidal: given two object Ci = (Gi, Bi, Ci), the product
C1 × C2 is (G1 × G2, B1 × B2, C1 ⊔C2) where the fiber of C1 ⊔C2 over (ϕ1, ϕ2) ∈B1 × B2 is the
disjoint union Cϕ1 ⊔Cϕ2. The initial object, which is ∅-stratified (see Definition 2.10), is the
object with G being the trivial group, B being the singleton, and C being empty.
Notice that the category Curve also allows outercollaring.
14.2.1. Lateral lines. We introduce the notation of lateral lines, which are used for gluing domains
of solutions to Floer equations in various flow-category constructions.
Definition 14.8.
(1) Let Σ be a smooth genus zero curve with cylindrical ends. A lateral
line in Σ is a real curve R ∼= ℓ⊆Σ satisfying: near ±∞, ℓis contained in a cylindrical end
of Σ and in cylindrical coordinates, is parametrized by (s, 0). In particular, for domains
of Floer trajectories, this amounts to an R-orbit of the translation action on the cylinder.
(2) Let C = (G, B, C) be an equivariant family of curves equipped with marked points and
cylindrical ends. A family of lateral lines is a collection of lateral lines ℓϕ ⊆Cϕ such that
ℓgϕ = g(ℓϕ) for all ϕ ∈B and g ∈G.
By abuse of notations, we use Curve to denote the category of equivariant family of curves
equipped with cylindrical ends and lateral lines.
It is easy to see that lateral lines can be
concatenated (as we always require the angular coordinate to be 0 ∈S1), which also guarantees
their existence by induction.
14.2.2. Rigidifications. To fit into the construction of Kuranishi charts and FOP perturbations, we
need to introduce the more refined category Curverig. Comparing to other “rigidified” morphisms
in categories such as dOrb or Kur, the difference between the domain and target of an embedding
in Curve is caused typically by the enlargement of the complex Lie group. So the rigidification
becomes more specific.
Definition 14.9. Let ζ21 : (G1, B1, C1) →(G2, B2, C2) be a strict embedding in Curve. A
rigidification of ζ21 consists of an orthogonal representation Q21 of G1 and a germ of ζG
21-
equivariant maps
θ21 : B1 × Qϵ

(where G1 acts diagonally on the domain) whose restriction to B1 × {0} coincides with ζB
21, such
that the map

A strict rigidified
embedding is a strict embedding together with a rigidification.

14.2.3. Stable complex structure. We would like to define a category CurveC of equivariant

Definition 14.12. A stable complex structure on an equivariant family of curves C = (G, B, C)
is a G-equivariant stable complex structure of the tangent bundle TB (see Definition 3.17). Recall
that it consists of a G-equivariant complex vector bundle I →B and a G-equivariant stable
isomorphism

s→I.

where the first summand is complex. Hence a stable complex structure on C1 naturally induces a
stable complex structure on C2 via the strict embedding ζ21. One can see that the induced one
is invariant under unitary conjugations of strict embeddings. Hence one can make the following
definition.

structures. The category CurveC consists of objects being equivariant families of curves with

Remark 14.14. There is a refinement of the above stratification, namely, the stratification by
combinatorial types, and each stratum is preserved by the Gd-action.
14.3.3. Orientations. The smooth part of the moduli space M0,2(Pd, d) is a complex manifold,
which is canonically oriented. The interior of Bd has a natural S1-action by reparametrizing the
cylinders so that the quotient IntBd/S1 is identified with an open subset of M0,2(Pd, d). Hence
we can orient Bd by regarding the infinitesimal S1-direction as positively oriented.

in the category Curve such that they satisfy associativity. In this way, one obtains a monotone
flow category enriched in Curve.

14.3.7. Orientation and concatenation. Recall that each Bd is oriented in a canonical way. Hence
for d = d1 + d2, there is a product orientation on Bd1 × Bd2. As Gd is a complex Lie group,
Gd×Gd1×Gd2 (Bd1×Bd2) has the induced product orientation. On the other hand, it is a codimension
one boundary of Bd, hence has a corresponding boundary orientation.
Lemma 14.20. The product orientation and the boundary orientation coincide.
Proof. We only consider the interior of the moduli spaces Bd1 and Bd2. Recall that Bd1/S1 and
Bd2/S1 are open subsets of complex manifolds. There are also orientation-preserving gluing maps
Bd1/S1 × ∆× Bd2/S1 →Bd/S1
where ∆is a small disk of gluing parameters. By explicitly writing down the gluing map in terms
of representative and the effect of reparametrizations, one can see that the induced map
Bd1 × [0, ϵ) × Bd2 →Bd
is orientation preserving. Because all Bd are odd-dimensional, our convention on induced boundary
orientation leads to the conclusion.
□

14.4.3. Homotopies. Flow homotopies associated to a 1-parameter family of variations of domain
Floer data is indeed quite trivial. Indeed, let Σt, t ∈[0, 1] be a smooth family of Floer domains
with m negative ends and 1 positive end. Then there are m + 1 copies of the monotone flow
categories Dom corresponding to the m inputs and 1 output. Suppose Σt is equipped with a
smooth family of lateral lines. Then Σ0 and Σ1 correspond to monotone multimodules DomΣ0
and DomΣ1 over (Dom, . . . , Dom; Dom). Moreover, by allowing t to vary, one naturally obtains a
monotone homotopy DomH from DomΣ0 to DomΣ1. Upon choosing any outercollaring width, these
items become corresponding collared versions. The following proposition demonstrates that the
choices of rigidifications and stable complex structures can also be carried inductively.

15. Relatively Smooth AMS lifts
In this section we carry out perhaps the most complex part of the AMS construction. We
demonstrate how to lift an outercollaring of a Floer flow category F to ⌊SrelKurrig, the category
of (collared and rigidified) relatively smooth Kuranishi spaces. In fact, we will construct a lift
to ⌊Kurrig, the category of collared and rigidified topological Kuranishi spaces. The lift to
relatively smooth version is automatic. The collared-and-rigidified feature is always preserved in
the construction as it is a basic prerequisite for inductively construct perturbations.
15.1. Integral modification of topological energy. We generalize the method of Rezchikov
[Rez22] of modifying the topological energy. First, there is a way to modify the symplectic form.

15.2. Framed maps. In this subsection, we describe a suitable package describing flow categories,
multimodules, and homotopies of infinite-dimensional spaces of maps into the target symplectic
manifold.

We prove that the footprint map is injective. Suppose (ϕ, u, F, 0) and (ϕ′, u′, F ′, 0) are sent to

As in the same Gpq-orbit there can only be one normalized Gpq-oribt, it follows that g ∈Gpq.
This implies that the footprint map is injective.

Definition 15.22. Let G be a Lie group.
(1) A µ-representation of a Lie group G is a direct system of complex representations
(Wµ)µ≥1 of G where Wµ →Wµ+1 is an embedding. A µ-representation is said to be
exhaustive if any finite-dimensional representation R of G can be embedded into Wµ for
µ sufficiently large.
(2) A single representation W is regarded as a µ-representation with Wµ ≡W.
(3) A µ-representation is called finite-dimensional if all Wµ are finite-dimensional; is called
unitary if all Wµ are equipped with G-invariant Hermitian inner products and the
embeddings Wµ →Wµ+1 are isometric.

equivariant linear isometric embeddings Wµ →W ′

15.4. Relatively smooth AMS lifts. The topological AMS lifts actually admit refinement in
the so-called relatively smooth category (see [HS22]). The discussion in this category is important
for eventually obtaining smooth Kuranishi spaces. We introduce the category of Kuranishi spaces
in this version.
15.4.1. Relative spaces and relative smooth structures.
Definition 15.27 (Relative spaces).
(1) A (stratified) relative space consists of a pair of A-stratified topological spaces Y , S
and a stratified continuous map π : Y →S. When π is understood from the context, we
denote a relative space by Y/S.


## Page 145

(2) A relative map from Y/S to Z/T is a commutative diagram
Y
/

Z

π(Y )
/ π(Z)
.
When S = T, without further declaration, we will assume that the underlying map from
π(Y ) to π(Z) is the restriction of the identity map of S.
(3) A relative homeomorphism is a relative map φ : Y/S →Z/T consisting of a homeo-
morphism from Y to Z and a homeomorphism from S to T.
(4) If G is a topological group, a G-action on relative space is a continuous action via relative
homeomorphisms. In particular, the map π : Y →S is equivariant.
(5) Let D be another topological space. The horizontal stabilization of a relative space
Y/S by D is the relative space
Stabhor
D (Y/S) = (Y × D)/(S × D).
The vertical stabilization of Y/S is the relative space
Stabvert
D (Y/S) = (Y × D)/S.
Notice that both horizontal and vertical stabilizations are functors on the categories of
relative spaces. Moreover, given D1, D2, there is a natural transformation
Stabhor

D2 ◦Stabhor
D1 .
Remark 15.28. A fiber bundle is a canonically a relative space. In particular, we denote by Rk/S
the relative space associated to the trivial Rk bundle over S.
Definition 15.29. Let U/S ⊆Rm/S and V/T ⊆Rn/T be open subsets. A continuous map
φ : U/S →V/T is called rel-C∞,k if the map F : U →Rn, determined by
φ(v, x) = (F(v, x), φ(x))
has all partial derivatives in v ∈Rm and those up order k varies continuously in x ∈S uniformly
on compact subsets of Rm. A rel-C∞,k-map is called a rel-C∞,k-diffeomorphism if it has a
rel-C∞,k-inverse. φ is called relatively smooth, or rel-C∞, if it is rel-C∞,k for all k.
Definition 15.30. A relative smooth structure on Y/S consists of an atlas φα : Uα/S →Rn/S
which are rel-C∞-compatible. More precisely 1) Y = ∪Uα and 2) for any α, β, the map
φα ◦φ−1
β
: φβ((Uα ∩Uβ)/S) →φα((Uα ∩Uβ)/S)
is a rel-C∞-diffeomorphism.
Notice that if Y/S has a relative smooth structure, then each fiber of Y has a well-defined smooth
structure. Then there is a well-defined rel-C∞vector bundle
T vertY/S →Y,
called the vertical tangent bundle.

15.4.2. The category of relative smooth Kuranishi spaces.
Definition 15.31 (Category of relatively smooth Kuranishi spaces).
(1) A relative smooth Kuranishi space is a quadruple K/B = (G, V/B, E, S) where
(G, V, E, S) is a topological Kuranishi space, B is a smooth G-manifold, together with a
G-invariant rel-C∞structure on V/B.
(2) A stabilization of a relative smooth Kuranishi space K/B by a G-equivariant disk bundle
D = Dhor ⊕Dvert ⊆N hor ⊕N vert
is the relative Kuranishi space
StabD(K/B) = (G, ˜V/ ˜B, ˜E, ˜S)
where ˜V/ ˜B is the stabilization of the relative space V/B by D (which carries a canonically
induced relative smooth structure), ˜E and ˜S are the natural stabilization.
(3) A strict embedding of relative smooth Kuranishi spaces from K1/B1 = (G1, V1/B1, E1, S1)
to K2/B2 = (G2, V2/B2, E2, S2) consists of a strict embedding
ι21 : K1 →K2
(which includes a group embedding ιG
21 : G1 →G2) and an ιG
21-equivariant smooth
embedding B1 →B2.
(4) An embedding of relative Kuranishi spaces from K1/B1 to K2/B2 is a conjugacy class
of strict embeddings.
(5) Let SrelKur be the category of relatively smooth Kuranishi spaces whose morphisms are
embeddings. One can also consider the rigidified and collared versions of this category.
Notice that there is an obvious functor
SrelKur →Kur
by sending
(G, V/B, E, S) 7→(G, V, E, S).
Proposition 15.32 (Relative smooth structures on AMS lifts).
(1) In Situation Flow(i), any topological AMS lift of the Floer flow category is canonically
lifted to ⌊SrelKurrig.
(2) In Situation Module(i), let ˆX be the AMS lift of the outercollaring of X to ⌊Kurrig as a
multimodule over the topological AMS lifts (ˆF1, . . . , ˆFm; ˆF′) enriched in ⌊Kurrig. Then ˆX
is canonically lifted to ⌊SrelKurrig.
(3) In Situation Homotopy(i), let ˆH be a topological AMS lift of the outercollaring of H to
⌊Kurrig. Then ˆH is canonically enriched in ⌊SrelKurrig.
Proof. We only discuss the case about the flow category. The other cases can be proved in the
same manner. For each pair p < q of objects in F, the fiberwise smooth structure of ⌊Vpq →⌊Bpq
follows from the implicit function theorem. The fact that these fiberwise structures vary in the
rel-C∞sense can be proved using the method of [Swa19] (which treats the case of Gromov–Witten
theory). Alternatively, it follows from the proof of [BX22a, Proposition 6.2].
□

16. Vertical Normal Complex Structures and orientations
16.1. Main statements about normal complex structures. Recall that one has defined the
category of relatively smooth Kuranishi spaces SrelKur. Given any object K/B = (G, V/B, E, S),
one has a well-defined vertical tangent bundle
T vtV →V
which is a G-equivariant relatively smooth vector bundle. Recall the definition of equivariant
normal complex structures (Definition 3.23). Given a relatively smooth Kuranishi space K/B =
(G, V/B, E, S), a relative NC structure on K/B is by definition a G-equivariant NC structure
on T vtV →V . One can then define the category of relative NC Kuranishi spaces, denoted by
SrelKurNC.
We can also consider the rigidified and collared versions. We omit the detailed definitions.
Now we state our main theorems about the existence of fiberwise stable complex structures of
Floer flow categories, multimodules, and homotopies.

denoted temporarily by ˆˆF, to ⌊SrelKurNC
rig . We call such a lift a relatively NC AMS lift.

16.5. Orientations. We explain how to obtain coherent orientations on the AMS construction.
We give orientations on the tangent bundles of the domain moduli spaces as well as the vertical
tangent bundles. After the stable smoothing to be done in the next section, they induce coherent
orientations on the AMS lifts.
We first look at the operator Dp given by (16.1). Its Fredholm index is congruent (mod 2) to the
cohomological degree of the periodic orbit p, denoted by |p|. Let det Dp be its real determinant
line.
Consider the flow category case. For each pair of objects p, q, one can use the 1-parameter family
of operators (16.3) to obtain an interpolation; in particular an isomorphism between determinant
lines
det Dp ⊗det Du ∼= det(DC
u ⊕Dq) ∼= det Dq.
The last isomorphism comes from the fact that DC
u is complex. We may regard the orientation of
the moduli space as the family of isomorphismss on the determinant lines
det Du : det Dp →det Dq.

17. Stable Smoothings
We proceed with the last step of constructing the AMS lifts. The original argument of such
abstract smoothing of moduli spaces was contained in [AMS21]. The inductive argument in the
setting of Floer theories was first systematically discussed in [BX22a].
17.1. Main results about stable smoothings. In the construction we will frequently stabilize
thickened moduli spaces obtained in the AMS construction. To simplify the discussion, we always
stabilize by trivial complex vector bundles. We make the following formal definition.
Definition 17.1. The category Kur+ has objects being (K, R) where K = (G, V, E, S) is a
Kuranishi space and R is a finite-dimensional unitary complex representation of G. A strict
embedding from (K1, R1) to (K2, R2) consists of a strict embedding of Kuranishi spaces ι21 :
K1 →K2 and an ιG
21-equivariant isometric linear embedding ιR
21 : R1 →R2. Morphisms of Kur+
are unitary conjugacy classes of strict embeddings.
Similarly, one can consider the collared and rigidified versions. Then there are two natural functors
from Kur+ to Kur: the first is to forget the representation R; the other is to take the stabilization
by R. What we would consider is the version
⌊SrelKur+
rig
for collared, rigidified, relatively smooth Kuranishi spaces.
Definition 17.2. A stabilizer of a flow category/multimodule/homotopy enriched in Kur is a
lift to Kur+.
Remark 17.3. In the AMS construction, a stabilizer can be viewed as adding trivial pieces to the
thickening data.
Notice that there are two natural functors from ⌊SrelKur+
rig to ⌊SrelKurrig. The first is to forget
the stabilizer. The second is to stabilize each Kuranishi space by the corresponding trivial vector
bundle via the vertical stabilization (see Definition 15.27). We often freely switch between the
stabilizer, which is a system of representations, and the corresponding vertical stabilization, viewed
as total space of the corresponding product equivariant vector bundles.

is a lift to ⌊SKurrig. A stable smoothing of a flow category/multimodule/homotopy enriched
in ⌊Kurrig consists of a lift to ⌊Kur+
rig and a smoothing of the corresponding stabilization.
Theorem 17.5. (Stable Smoothing Theorem)
(1) In Situation Flow(i), suppose ˆF is a relatively smooth AMS lift of F subject to a certain
outercollaring width. Then there exists a stable smoothing of ˆF. In other words, there
exists a vertical stabilization ˆF+ and a smoothing of ˆF+. We still denote the flow category
enriched in ⌊SKurrig by ˆF+ and call it an AMS stable smoothing of ˆF.

17.2. Preliminaries.
17.2.1. Microbundles. Recall that a microbundle of rank k over a topological space M, denoted
by F, is a topological space F together with two structural maps p : F →M, i : M →F such
that p ◦i = IdM and such that near the image of i, locally F is an Rk-bundle. Any real vector
bundle F →M together with the bundle projection and the zero section induces a microbundle
Fµ. If M is a topological manifold, then its tangent microbundle TµM has the total space M ×M
with p the projection onto the first factor and i the diagonal embedding of M.
A morphism of microbundles over M from F to F ′ is a germ of continuous maps from a
neighborhood of i(M) ⊆F to F ′ which commutes with the structural maps. A morphism is
called an isomorphism if it can be represented by a homeomorphism. An isotopy of microbundle
isomorphisms from ϕ0 : F →F ′ to ϕ1 : F →F ′ is a microbundle isomorphism from
˜
F →˜
F ′
over M × [0, 1] whose restriction to M × {i} coincides with ϕi (as germs) for i = 0, 1.
In particular, a vector bundle reduction of a microbundle F →M consists of a vector bundle
F →M and a microbundle isomorphism Fµ →F. When a topological manifold M is equipped
with a smooth structure, denoted by Mα, then there is a vector bundle reduction (TMα)µ →TµM:
a concrete choice could be the exponential map associated to a Riemannian metric. The isotopy
class of such a vector bundle reduction is canonical. Hence it is a necessary condition for a
topological manifold to admit a smooth structure that its tangent microbundle admits a vector
bundle reduction.
All the above notions admit equivariant extensions with respect to actions by compact Lie groups.
In particular, when a topological G-manifold M is equipped with a smooth structure Mα such
that the G-action is smooth, then there is a well-defined isotopy class of G-equivariant vector
bundle reduction (TMα)µ →TµM.
Remark 17.6. We need the notion of smooth microbundles over smooth manifolds. These are
microbundles whose total spaces and structural maps are all in the C∞category. One can also
define smooth microbundle maps and smooth vector bundle reductions. Equivariant versions


## Page 165


INTEGRAL HAMILTONIAN FLOER THEORY
165
can be formulated as well. For example, when M is already a smooth manifold, the tangent
microbundle is smooth and any vector bundle reduction of TµM using a smooth Riemannian
metric is smooth.
17.2.2. Stable G-smoothings. Now we recall notions about equivariant smoothings. Let M be
a topological manifold with an action by a compact Lie group G. A G-smoothing of M is a
smooth structure α on M such that the G-action is smooth. Two G-smoothings α0 and α1 are
called equivalent if the identity map Mα0 →Mα1 is a diffeomorphism; they are called isotopic
if the identity map is homotopic through G-equivariant homeomorphisms to a diffeomorphism.
More generally, a stable G-smoothing of M consists of an orthogonal G-representation R and
a G-smoothing on M × R. Notice that given any stable G-smoothing (M × R)α and any other
orthogonal G-representation R′, the product smooth structure (M ×R)α ×R′ gives another stable
G-smoothing. Two stable G-smoothings (M × R1)α1 and (M × R2)α2 are stably isotopic if
there exist orthogonal G-representations R′
1, R′
2 such that R1 ⊕R′
1 ∼= R2 ⊕R′
2 as orthogonal
G-representations and such that the two G-smoothings (M × R1)α1 × R′
1 and (M × R2)α2 × R′
2
on M × R1 × R′
1 = M × R2 × R′
2 are isotopic.
Notice that given a G-smoothing Mα on M, there is an induced isotopy class of G-equivariant
vector bundle reductions (TMα)µ →TµM. In view of stable smoothings, one also need to define
the notion of stable isotopy of stable vector bundle reductions.
Definition 17.7. [Las79, Page 300] Let F →M be a G-microbundle. A stable G-vector
bundle reduction of F consists of a G-vector bundle F and an orthogonal G-representation R
together with a G-microbundle isomorphism
ϕ : Fµ →F ⊕Rµ.
Two stable G-vector bundle reductions ϕi : (Fi)µ →F ⊕(Ri)µ, i = 0, 1, are called stably
isotopic if there exist orthogonal G-representations R′
0, R′
1 such that R0 ⊕R′
0 ∼= R1 ⊕R′
1 and the
stabilizations ϕ0⊕IdR′
0 and ϕ1⊕IdR′
1 are isotopic vector bundle reductions of F ⊕(R0)µ⊕(R′
0)µ ∼=
F ⊕(R1)µ ⊕(R′
1)µ.
Remark 17.8. In the above discussions, we can replace “orthogonal representations” by “unitary
representations” to suit our purpose. Any orthogonal representation can be stabilized to a unitary
representation.
Now given any stable G-smoothing (M × R)α of M, there is an isotopy class of G-vector bundle
reductions T(M × R)α →Tµ(M × R). The restriction of this reduction to M × {0} gives a stable
isotopy class of G-vector bundle reductions of TµM. Lashof’s theorem says that this association
has an inverse.
Theorem 17.9. [Las79] Let M be a topological G-manifold with finitely many orbit types. Then
the above map which sends each stable isotopy class of stable G-smoothings to a stable isotopy
class of G-vector bundle reductions of TµM is invertible. In particular, if TµM admits a G-vector
bundle reduction, then M admits a stable G-smoothing.
17.2.3. Micro-connections. Let π : V →B be a G-equivariant relatively smooth manifold such
that B is a smooth G-manifold. Then there is a well-defined G-microbundle over V :
π∗TµB ⊕T vt
µ V.
To facilitate the stable smoothing of V , we need to identify the above microbundle with the
tangent microbundle of V . Notice that there are natural microbundle maps
T vt

Hence such an identification can be regarded as a microbundle version of connections.
Definition 17.10. Let V/B be a G-equivariant topological submersion. A micro-connection is
a G-equivariant microbundle map ∇µ : TµV →T vt
µ V satisfying
(1) The restriction of ∇µ to T vt
µ V is the identity map.
(2) ∇µ is represented by a germ of the map ϕ : ∆+(V ) →V such that for any (b, b′) ∈
(π×π)(∆+(V )) ⊆B×B and p ∈π−1(b) ⊆V , the restriction of ϕ to ∆+(V )∩({p}×π−1(b′))
is a homeomorphism onto an open neighborhood of (p, p) in {p} × π−1(b)).
Notice that a micro-connection induces a microbundle isomorphism TµM ∼= π∗TµB ⊕T vt
µ M.
Lemma 17.11. Let C ⊆V be a G-invariant closed subset and U ⊆V be a G-invariant open
neighborhood of C. Let D ⊆V be another G-invariant closed set. Suppose ∇µ : TµU →T vt
µ U is
a G-invariant micro-connection. Then there exists a G-invariant open neighborhood W of C ∪D
and a G-invariant micro-connection ∇′

Proof. This is a restatement of the extension lemma [AMS21, Lemma 4.24] and a relative version
of [AMS21, Proposition 4.25].

17.3. Pre-smoothed relatively smooth Kuranishi spaces. To describe the structures one
needs to perform the stable smoothing, we enhance the category of relatively smooth Kuranishi
spaces to include various specific add-ons.
Definition 17.12. Let K = (G, V/B, E, S) be an object of ⌊SrelKurrig.
(1) A pre-smoothing on K consists of
(a) A G-invariant Riemannian metric gT B on B and a G-invariant metric hT vtV on
T vtV .
(b) A micro-connection ∇µ on V/B.
(2) A pre-smoothing is called collared if for each stratum Vα/Bα, the following holds.
(a) Near Bα, gT B is the product of gT Bα and the standard metric on the collar.
(b) Near Vα, hT vtV is constant in collar coordinates.
(c) Near Vα, ∇µ factors as
TµV
/ TµVα
/ T vt
µ Vα
where the first arrow is induced from the collar structure and the second arrow is
the restriction of the micro-connection on the stratum Vα/Bα.
(3) A pre-smoothed Kuranishi space is a relatively smooth Kuranishi space equipped with a
pre-smoothing.
(4) A stabilization of a pre-smoothed Kuranishi space K by a trivial G-equivariant disk bundle
D = Dhor ⊕Dvt is the stabilized Kuranishi space StabDK together with the naturally
induced pre-smoothing (where Dhor and Dvt are equipped with linear metrics).

O. The signs come from the properties of the orientations on the domain moduli spaces (see

18.3. Proof of Theorem Q. The proof is simply a different organization of the previous
constructions for flow categories and multimodules.
Notice that it is important to choose
integral actions which are compatible throughout the consideration. Then each stage of the AMS
construction, starting from the domain flow category, rigidifications, choosing thickening data
and group reductions, constructing relative NC structures, stable smoothing, and smooth NC
structures, can all be inductively accomplished. The statement of Theorem Q then follows without
any new difficulties than flow category and multimodule cases.

Proposition 19.2. In Situation Concatenation(i), let
⌊DomF1, . . . , ⌊DomFm, ⌊DomG1, . . . , ⌊DomGn, ⌊DomG′
be the monotone flow categories enriched in ⌊CurveC
rig subject to the same outercollaring width. Let
⌊DomΣX resp. ⌊DomΣY be monotone multimodules over (⌊DomF1, . . . , ⌊DomFm; ⌊DomGi) resp. over
(⌊DomG1, . . . , ⌊DomGn; ⌊DomG′) constructed in Subsection 14.4. Then there exists a concatenation

Proof. The underlying monotone multimodules enriched in Curve have a canonical concatenation.
Indeed, let ΣX◦iY be the singular curve with two components ΣX and ΣY glued along the cylindrical
end of the concatenation. Notice that the lateral lines in ΣX and ΣY glue together to lateral lines
in the singular domain. Then the same construction of DomΣ can be applied to ΣX◦iY, providing
a monotone multimodule DomΣX ◦i DomΣY. It is straightforward to check that the outercollaring
of DomΣX ◦i DomΣY is also a concatenation of the corresponding outercollarings of DomΣX and
DomΣY.

19.2. Relatively smooth AMS lifts for concatenations and gluing. In this subsection we
treat the topological AMS construction for concatenations of Floer multimodules associated to
smooth Floer domains.
19.2.1. Concatenation. Assume we are in Situation Concatenation(i).
We have constructed
relatively smooth AMS lifts ˆX and ˆY to ⌊SrelKurrig respectively. The purpose of this subsection
is to demonstrate how to extend the constructions of ˆX and ˆY to a lift of the concatenation X ◦i Y
to ⌊SrelKurrig.
Theorem 19.4. In Situation Concatenation(i), let ˆX and ˆY be relatively smooth AMS lifts of the
outercollarings of X and Y respectively, subject to the same outercollaring width and integral actions
which induce the same integral symplectic form on X. Then there exists a set of concatenations
ˆX ◦i ˆY enriched in ⌊SrelKurrig.

19.2.2. Gluing.
Theorem 19.5. In Situation Glue(i), fix an outercollaring width and integral actions on the
involved flow categories compatible with respect to H. Let ˆX and ˆY be relatively smooth AMS lifts
of X and Y subject to the outercollaring width and the integral actions. Let ˆX◦ˆY be a concatenation
provided by Theorem 19.4. Let ˆM be an AMS lift of the multimodule M. Then there exists a set of

canonically obtained.

20. Comparing Non-homotopic Choices
The comparison between different choices which are not homotopic requires more setup. Choices
in this category include
(1) The choice of integral actions (see Definition 15.1) in construction the AMS lifts.
(2) Different choices of lateral lines in Floer domains.
We remove the dependence on these two choices uniformly via a doubling construction (cf. [AMS21,
Section 6.10]). Here, different integral actions give rise to different moduli spaces of curves of
projective spaces; as for different choices of lateral lines under the same choice of integral actions,
they induce different composition laws. Despite the different effects, we can handle them in the
same way.

flow categories/multimodules/homotopies (see Definition 14.1, Definition 14.4, Definition 14.5)

21. AMS Construction for Other Flow categories, Multimodules, and Homotopies
In this section we discuss the AMS construction for flow categories etc. which are not from smooth
Floer domains.
21.1. Pearly flow category. Recall that the Pearly flow category P is associated to an almost
K¨ahler manifold (X, ω, J) and a Morse–Smale pair (f, g) on X. The object set is
ObP = critf × Π
where Π =
π2(X)
kerc1 ∩kerω .
An object is written as x = (x, ax) where x ∈critf and ax ∈Π. We will construct AMS lifts of
outercollarings of P as declared in Theorem 12.7. The main difference from the case of the Floer
flow category is that the boundary strata of moduli spaces are given by fiber products instead of
direct products.
21.1.1. Fibered topological spaces, Kuranishi spaces, and derived orbifolds.

(2) Let Kur(Y ) be the regular stratification category whose objects are (K, f−, f+) where
K = (G, V, E, S) is a Kuranishi space and f± : V →Y is a pair of G-invariant topological
submersions. Let SKur(Y ) be the corresponding category of smooth Kuranishi spaces
together with smooth submersions to Y . Let SKurNC(Y ) be the subcategory whose
objects are smooth normally complex Kuranishi spaces with G-invariant submersions. Let
SrelKur(Y ) be the category of relatively smooth Kuranishi spaces K/B = (G, V/B, E, S)
together with G-invariant maps f± : V →Y such that they are fiberwise submersions.
Let SrelKurNC(Y ) be the subcategory where the relative Kuranishi spaces are equipped
with relative NC structures.
(3) Let dOrb(Y ) be the category whose objects are pairs (D, f−, f+) where D = (U, E, S)
is a derived orbifold and f± : U →Y are smooth submersions. Let dOrbNC(Y ) be the
category whose objects are (D, f−, f+) where D is an NC derived orbifold and f± : U →Y
are smooth submersions.
Remark 21.2. Unlike the presumed stable complex versions, the monoidal structure given by fiber
products in the NC versions (SKurNC(Y ), SrelKurNC(Y ), dOrbNC(Y )) does not require the
almost complex structure on Y because Y is assumed to be a manifold.
Notice that one can also talk about collared and rigidified versions of Kur(Y ), SKur(Y ), and
dOrb(Y ).
One needs to generalize the discussion of stable smoothings of topological Kuranishi spaces to the
category Kur(Y ).
Definition 21.3. A stable smoothing of an object (K, f±) ∈Kur(Y ) where K = (G, V, E, S)
consists of a unitary representation W of G, a G-smoothing on V × W, a G-invariant extension
˜f± : V × W →Y of f± : V ∼= V × {0} →Y such that ˜f± are smooth submersions.
Lemma 21.4 (Stable smoothing with maps). (cf. [AMS21, Lemma 4.5]) Let V be a smooth
G-manifold and f0 : V →Y be a continuous G-invariant map. Then there exists a unitary
representation W of G, a G-smoothing on V × W which is in the same stable isotopy class of
the given smooth structure on V , and a smooth submersion ˜f : V × W →Y whose restriction to
V × {0} coincides with f0.
Proof. The proof is essentially that of [AMS21, Lemma 4.5], which we reproduce using our
language.
Choose a Riemannian metric on Y .
Then one can choose a smooth G-invariant
approximation f : V →Y and a smooth G-invariant function ϵ : V →R+ such that for all v ∈V ,
there is a unique shortest geodesic of length shorter than ϵ(v) connecting f0(v) and f(v), and such
that the exponential map expf(v) ξ is well-defined for ξ in the ϵ(v)-ball of Tf(v)Y . Then there is a
smooth equivariant bundle map ρ : f ∗TY →f ∗TY compressing f ∗TY diffeomorphically into the
ϵ-disk bundle. Therefore the map
˜f : f ∗TY →Y, ˜f(v, ξ) = expf(v)(ρ(ξ)).
is a smooth submersion. Choose another smooth (real) G-vector bundle E →V such that
E ⊕f ∗TY ∼= V × W
for some unitary representation W of G. ˜f clearly extends to a smooth G-equivariant map from
V × W to Y , which is still a smooth submersion.
We would like to define a self-homeomorphism of V × W to pullback the smooth structure on
V × W. Indeed, we can write
f0(v) = expf(v) ξ0(v), ξ0(v) ∈Tf(v)Y.

Proof of Theorem 12.7. We first describe the lift of the pearly flow category P. Choose a lift
c
cyl(X) provided by Proposition 21.5. By taking quotient, one obtains a monotone flow category
enriched in ⌊dOrbNC
rig (X), which induces an ordinary flow category, denoted by f
Cyl(X), whose
objects are elements of Π.
Then one can obtain a lift of the corresponding outercollaring of P by “inserting flow lines.” We
do this inductively. In the lowest energy case, one only needs to add flow rays at the negative
and positive ends to obtain Kuranishi charts of moduli spaces labelled by a pair of critical points.
In higher energy case, there are configurations where in the interior there are gradient segments
of positive length. An inductive scheme can be designed in a rather elementary way to produce
inductively all charts for involved moduli spaces. Hence one obtains a lift of the outercollaring of
P to ⌊dOrbNC
rig (inserting flow lines does not affect the NC structure).
Next, we discuss the regularization of the bimodule ∆PP. In fact, one can take different Morse–
Smale pairs and different almost complex structures to define P and consider the bimodule as an
interpolation between them. However, we choose the same one to simplify the notations.
The construction is very similar to the regularization of the Pearly flow category. First, there is a
monotone bimodule ∆cyl(X) given by holomorphic cylinders which have a parametrized principal
component. This is a monotone bimodule over (cyl(X); cyl(X)).
Then after outercollaring, following the same procedure as the proof of Proposition 21.5, one
obtains an AMS lift to ⌊SKurNC
rig (X), denoted by b∆cyl(X). After taking quotient, one obtains
a bimodule e∆cylP(X) over (f
cyl(X); f
cyl(X)) enriched in ⌊dOrbNC
rig (X). Then using the fact that
evaluation maps are smooth submersions, by inserting Morse flow lines, one obtains a lift of ⌊∆PP
as a bimodule over (˜P; ˜P). This proves the second item of Theorem 12.7.
□

Remark 21.7. In [BX22a] one considered the Morse-to-Floer and Floer-to-Morse bimodules and
their concatenation along the Floer flow category. However, their concatenation along the Morse
flow category is difficult to regularize (the codimension two gluing is hard to fit into the framework
of flow bimodules). In this paper, we do not consider these two bimodules and their concatenations.
The Morse-to-Morse bimodule will arise from the concatenations of the Morse-to-Pearly and the
Pearly-to-Morse bimodules, whose regularization will be addressed later.

Part 4. EQUIVARIANT FLOER THEORY AND STEENROD OPERATIONS
Outline of Part 4
Note that we would like to switch to the cohomological convention. The Floer equation will
remain the same. However, it will be the equation for the ascending gradient flow equation of
the action functional (which differs from the one used previously by a negative sign). Hence the
differential increases the action functional. The grading is also changed to the cohomological one
so that the pair-of-pants product is degree-preserving. Moreover, we make use of the coherent
orientations without explicitly mentioning them.

23. Equivariant Morse and Floer Cohomology
The purpose of this section is to discuss both an algebraic and Morse-theoretic approach to
equivariant cohomology.
The former is used for constructing the quasi-Frobenius map (cf.
Equation (23.3)) and the latter uses the Morse flow category discussed in the previous section to
define the θ-action on equivariant cohomology.
23.1. Tate Equivariant Cohomology.
23.1.1. The algebraic construction. Fix a prime p. The coefficient ring will be Fp unless otherwise
declared. Let D be a Zp-equivariant cochain complex with differential dD. Let τ : D →D be the
action of the generator τ ∈Zp. Define cochain complexes of Kp-modules
CZp(D) := D ⊗eKp ∼=
(
D ⊗F2((θ)),
p = 2,
D ⊗Fp((U))⟨θ⟩,
p > 2.
where the differential dZp is defined as follows.
(1) When p = 2, dZp is θ-linear and is determined by
dZp(x ⊗θk) = dD(x) ⊗θk + (1 −τ)(x) ⊗θk+1.
(23.1)
(2) When p > 2, dZp is U-linear and is determined by
dZp(x ⊗1) = dD(x) ⊗1 + (−1)|x|(1 −τ)(x) ⊗θ,
dZp(x ⊗θ) = dD(x) ⊗θ + (−1)|x|(1 + τ + · · · + τ p−1)(x) ⊗U.
(23.2)
Here |x| is the cohomological grading of x.
The resulting cohomology, called the (Tate) equivariant cohomology of D, is denoted by
HZp(D)
which is naturally a Kp-module. In fact the equivariant cohomology HZp(D) can be made a
module over eKp in a non-obvious way (unless p = 2). We choose to construct the module structure
in the Morse-theoretic approach. We will not use the above algebraic definition formally, but only
provide it for algebraic intuition.
Remark 23.1. If we work with D ⊗Kp equipped with the same differentials (23.1) and (23.2),
then we obtain the Borel-equivariant cohomology. We mostly stick with the Tate setting because
of intended applications.
The equivariant complex is functorial. Namely, if f : D →D′ is a Zp-equivariant chain map, then
it induces a chain map
fZp : CZp(D) →CZp(D′).
If f0 and f1 are Zp-equivariantly chain homotopic, then there is a corresponding Kp-linear chain
homotopy from f0,Zp to f1,Zp.

23.2. Zp-equivariant Morse complexes.
23.2.1. Equivariant Morse cohomology for Zp-manifolds. Let M be a compact oriented manifold
equipped with a Zp-action. Let f : M →R be a Zp-invariant Morse function. One would like to
define a Zp-equivariant Morse cochain complex CMZp(f).
First we assume that there exists a Zp-invariant Riemannian metric g on M making (f, g) Morse–
Smale. Namely, one can achieve equivariant transversality. Then one obtains a cochain complex
CM(f, g) which has a Zp-action. Hence the previous algebraic construction provides a way to
obtain an equivariant Morse complex
CMZp(f) := CZp(CM ∗(f, g)).
In fact the equivariant complex can also be obtained via Morse model. Consider the product
function
F : M × bS∞→R, F(x, w) = f(x) + fE(w)
and the product metric, which provides a Morse flow category M#E. Consider the associated
Morse cochain complex
CM(F)
which coincides with the tensor product CM(f) ⊗E. This complex has a Zp-action given by the
Zp-action on CM(f) and the Zp-action on E. Then define
CMZp(f) := (CM(f) ⊗E)Zp
i.e., the Zp-fixed part.

The two special cases mentioned above also arises in the Morse-theoretic context. First, suppose
Zp acts trivially on M. Then a Morse–Smale pair (f, g) is equivariantly transverse, resulting in
the identification
HMZp(f, g) ∼= HM(f, g) ⊗eKp.
On the other hand, if (f, g) is Morse–Smale on M, then the p-fold power
(f p, g⊕p)
is Morse–Smale on M p. One obtains the cochain complex
CM(f p) ⊗C(E)
whose Zp-invariant has cohomology HMZp(f p, g⊗p).
When M is compact, Morse-theoretic
technique proves that the resulting cohomology is independent of the choice of (f, g) and coincides
with HZp(M p).
Now assume that one does not have equivariant transversality. Let M be equipped with a
Zp-action, f : M →R is a Zp-invariant Morse function. Still consider the product
F : M × bS∞→R.
Now the Zp-action on M × bS∞is free, allowing us to achieve equivariant transversality. There
are many concrete choices of perturbations. For example, one can perturb the function f to
allow it to depend on w ∈bS∞, i.e., a Zp-invariant function (w, x) 7→fv(x) on bS∞× M such that
fv(x) = f(x) when (v, x) is close to a critical point of g ×f. One can further ensure that the set of
critical points is unchanged under the perturbation provided that the perturbation is sufficiently
small in C1-norm. Then one can consider the coupled flow line equation for (ξ, η) : R →M × bS∞:
η′(s) = ∇fζ(s)(η(s)),
ζ′(s) = ∇g(ζ(s)),
which has again an obvious translation symmetry. Define the Morse cochain complex as the
(completed) tensor product
CM(f) ⊗CM(g)
whose differential counts rigid perturbed flow lines. Define
CMZp(f) := (CM(f) ⊗CM(g))Zp
as a subcomplex, whose chain homotopy type is independent of the perturbation.
By using perturbed flow trees in bS∞, one can also define a module structure of HMZp(f) over
eKp ∼= HZp(pt) induced by a chain-level bilinear map
CMZp(f) ⊗Fp CMZp(g) →CMZp(f).

24. Quantum Steenrod Operations—Proof of Theorem H
The classical Steenrod operation can be viewed as a Zp-equivariant extension of the map x 7→xp
in cohomology in characteristic p. Proposed by Fukaya [Fuk97], the quantum Steenrod operation
deforms the classical one in analogy to the case that the quantum cup product deforms the
classical cup product. Under topological restrictions such as monotonicity, the quantum Steenrod
operation has been constructed by Seidel [Sei15a], Wilkins [Wil20], and Seidel–Wilkins [SW22]
(see also the recent survey by Wilkins [Wil24]).
The use of the FOP transverse perturbations allows us to define integer, hence Fp-counts of
pseudoholomorphic curves, leading to a definition of the quantum Steenrod operation on all
compact symplectic manifolds. In this section we go over the construction using the Morse model.
24.1. Geometric setup.

24.4. Comparing to Seidel–Wilkins’ definition in semi-positive case. When (X, ω) is
semi-positive, Seidel–Wilkins [SW22] defined their quantum Steenrod operations using the classical
approach. We prove that our definition agrees with theirs.
Theorem 24.6. When (X, ω) is semi-positive, the quantum Steenrod operation defined above
agrees with the definition of Seidel–Wilkins.
Remark 24.7. Strictly speaking, we are referring to the operations denoted by QSt instead of
QΣb in [SW22], where the latter is featured more in recent studies of arithmetic properties of
quantum connections on semi-positive symplectic manifolds (cf. [Che24b, Che24a], [Lee25, Lee23],
[BL25, BLP26]). We can extend our QStp to define analogs of QΣb using our framework and prove
that it reduces to the Seidel–Wilkins operation when (X, ω) is semi-positive. Moreover, such an
extension is covariantly constant under the quantum connection defined using FOP perturbations.
We will not discuss such extensions in this manuscript.

25. The Equivariant Floer theory for iterations—Proof of Theorem I
In this section we construct the equivariant Floer theory, including equivariant Floer cohomology,
equivariant continuation maps, and equivariant PSS/SSP maps, induced from a prime iteration of
a Hamiltonian. Let p be a prime.
25.1. Equivariant Floer flow category as a coupling.
25.1.1. Coupling of Floer and Morse flow categories. We define a notion called coupling of flow
categories. This notion cannot be defined on the abstract level but rely on the geometric setup.
Let F be the Floer flow category on X associated to a pair (H, J). Let M be the Morse flow
category on another manifold Y associated to a Morse–Smale pair (f, g). Define F#M be a flow
category as follows. The set of objects is
Ob(F#M) = ObF × ObM
whose elements are denoted by p = (pF, pM). For the morphism space M F#M
pq
, consider the moduli
space of pairs (u, v) where u : R × S1 →X is a solution to the Floer equation connection pF and
qF and v : R →Y is a Morse trajectory connection pM and qM; two pairs (u, v) and (u′, v′) are
equivalent if there exists s0 ∈R such that
u(·, ·) = u′(· + s0, ·),
v(·) = v′(· + s0).
One then compactifies this moduli spaces by allowing “synchronized” breakings (as well as sphere
bubblings).
Now we describe the equivariant Floer flow category as a special coupling.
Definition 25.1. Let H be a 1-periodic Hamiltonian on (X, ω) such that the p-th iteration
H♮p is nondegenerate. Let J be a fixed ω-compatible almost complex structure. Define the
Zp-equivariant Floer flow category to be the coupling
FZp := F#E
where E is the Morse flow category on bS∞.
On FZp there is an action
A = (A1, A2) : ObFZp →R2
where
A1(pF, pE) = AH♮p(pF),
A2(pF, pE) = f E(pE).
The grading is given by
deg(pF, pE) := deg(pF) + deg(pE) ∈Z.
Moreover, there is a free action on ObFZp the twisted Novikov group
Πp := Π × Z × Zp = Π × Zp.

26. The Equivariant Pair-of-Pants—Proof of Theorem J
The equivariant pair-of-pants product can be viewed as a Floer-theoretic analogue of the quantum
Steenrod operation. It is defined via a multimodule associated to a Floer domain having a
Zp-symmetry. The main results regarding such a construction is stated in Theorem J. Given the
recurring nature of our arguments, we highlight the distinctive features.
26.1. The equivariant pair-of-pants multimodule. Let (X, ω, J) be a compact almost K¨ahler
manifold. Again, we do not discuss the dependence on J, which could be done without new
difficulty. Let F be the Floer flow category of a Hamiltonian H and let FZp be the coupling of
the Floer flow category of the p-th iteration H♮p with the Morse flow category E on bS∞.27 We
describe a multimodule XFSt over (F, · · · , F, E; FZp) where F appears p times in the source.
26.1.1. The Floer domain and the equation. Let z1, . . . , zp ∈C ⊆CP1 be all the p-th roots of
unity. Let
Σ := CP1 \ {z1, . . . , zp, z∞= ∞}
be the punctured sphere, which has a holomorphic Zp-action permuting the finite punctures.
Choose a Zp-invariant Floer datum σZp on Σ whose restriction on the p negative ends are Hdt
and whose restriction on the positive end is the p-th iteration H♮pdt. In fact, one can choose
different H for negative and positive ends as in the setup of Theorem J, but we spell out the
argument for H−= H+ = H to simplify the notations. Solutions to the Floer equation
∂σZpu = 0, u : Σ →X
converge to periodic orbits of H at negative ends and periodic orbits of H♮p at the positive end.
26.1.2. The moduli spaces and the multimodule. We define the morphism spaces of the multimodule

26.2. Proof of Theorem J. As above, we write down the proof for H−= H+ = H to simplify
the notations.
26.2.1. Independence of choices.
Lemma 26.3. eP is well-defined up to Zp-equivariant chain homotopies. Therefore, restricting to
the Zp-invariant part, P is well-defined up to chain homotopies.
Proof. Two concrete chain level constructions can be connected by a homotopy: we can carry out
the construction of multimodule homotopies in the Zp-equivariant setting using families of curves
with parametrization having the additional factor from the moduli spaces of Morse trajectories in
bS∞, and the Zp-equivariance can be achieved due to the freeness.
□
Therefore, following the definition of FStp in Theorem J, we see that the Floer-theoretic Steenrod
operation does not depend on any choices after passing to cohomology. This finishes proving
Theorem J (1) and (3).
26.2.2. Isomorphism on cohomology. Theorem J (2) follows from a stronger assertion in the
quantitative setting, Corollary 27.10, which is proven in the next Section. The idea is that instead
of directly constructing a chain-homotopy inverse of P by dualizing the equivariant pair-of-pants
product, we turn on the action filtration on the (equivariant) Floer chain complexes, which
the operation P respects, so that the induced operation on the associated graded reduces to
the equivariant pants product on local Floer cohomology. Then, by appealing to the known
isomorphism property in [Sei15b, SZ21] in the aspherical setting, which, in particular, applies to
local Floer theory, we conclude that P is an isomorphism on cohomology.
Remark 26.4. Using Proposition 23.3, we further see that FStp is an isomorphism. This is the
generalization of the localization results in [Sei15b, SZ21] for general compact symplectic manifolds
in Hamiltonian Floer theory. We expect that the method in this paper can also be used for proving
an analogous result for the fixed point Floer cohomology associated with a general symplectic
automorphism, though one needs a slight different regularization procedure.

27. Quantitative Results about Equivariant Floer Theory
In this section we discuss the quantitative features of the equivariant Floer theory and prove
Theorem M.
27.1. Filtration on the p-fold tensor product. Let H be a nondegenerate Hamiltonian on X.
Let CF(H) be a Floer complex associated to H, obtained by a particular AMS construction and
FOP perturbation. The source of the equivariant pair-of-pants product is the complex
CZp(CF(H)⊗p)
which is the Zp-invariant part of
CF(H)⊗p ⊗CM(f E).
By our convention, this tensor product is a module over the Novikov field
ΛΠ⊕···⊕Π⊕Z
Fp
(where one has p copies of Π in the exponent). Here within this section, without introducing
further notations, we always change coefficients to the Novikov field ΛΠ
Kp using the summation
homomorphism Π ⊕· · · ⊕Π →Π. Then there is a filtration on
CF(H)⊗p ⊗CM(f E)
corresponding to the sum of the symplectic actions of the p tensor factors such that the differential
preserves this filtration. The filtration restricts to the Zp-invariant part. Moreover, after changing
coefficients to ΛΠ
Kp, one obtains a Floer-type complex over this Novikov field
 CF(H)⊗p ⊗CM(f E)


as well as its Zp-invariant part.
In principle one should hope to establish a chain-level inverse of the equivariant pair-of-pants
product via the coproduct construction (cf. [SZ21]). However, the corresponding AMS construction
is quite involved. Since our main purpose of the quantitative aspects is to compare barcodes,
instead of this potential direct approach, we use more algebraic treatment to bypass the coproduct
construction (although we will make use of the local coproduct construction in [SZ21], see the
proof of Lemma 27.11).

of Px for p > 2.
□

28. Quantitative Theory for Noncontractible Hamiltonian Orbits
In this section we provide constructions for the Floer complex for noncontractible orbits (in the
homological sense) and the corresponding quantitative results involving a version of equivariant
pair-of-pants product. As mentioned before, the main motivation for developing this variant is
to generalize the result of Sugimoto [Sug26] to full generality, which will be done in a separate
paper with Shelukhin and Wilkins [BSWX26]. Here in this section, we will prove Theorem 1.11
and Theorem 1.12. The only notable technical difference is that the capping of a non-contractible

orbit is defined relative to a reference homology class; the rest of the construction is exactly the
same as the contractible case.
28.1. The Floer flow category and bimodules. Let γ be a smooth loop in X which represents
a nontrivial class in H1(X; Z)free. Denote
Γ = H2(X; Z)/kerω.
For a nondegenerate Hamiltonian H, we describe a Z2-graded Γ-equivariant flow category Fγ of
orbits of H which are homologous to γ. Recall terms defined in Definition 1.10. Define
ObFγ := ˜Oγ(H).
To define morphisms of Fγ, choose a 1-periodic family of ω-compatible almost complex structures J
and considering moduli spaces of Floer equations and their compactifications. Then the morphism
set between two γ-capped orbits is the moduli space of stable Floer trajectories connecting them,
where the Floer cylinders naturally concatenate the γ-cappings. One then obtains the flow category
Fγ enriched in Top where objects are naturally Z2-graded. The action functional
AH,γ : ObFγ →R
makes Fγ a locally finite flow category (Definition 7.2). The Γ-action on objects makes Fγ a
Γ-equivariant flow category (Definition 7.24).
Notice that if we choose a different loop γ′ homologous to γ, then one essentially get the same
flow category except that the actions AH,γ and AH,γ′ differ by a constant.
If we choose a different pair (H′, J′) and consider the same construction (for a fixed loop γ), then
one obtains a similar Floer flow category F′
γ. By choosing a Floer datum on the infinite cylinder
interpolating between (H, J) and (H′, J′) and considering moduli spaces of Floer equations
associated to the Floer datum, one obtains a bimodule Bγ over (Fγ; F′
γ), which is naturally
Γ-equivariant (Definition 7.26). This is completely analogous to the usual contractible case.
28.2. The complex and continuation map. Since the Floer homology of the noncontractible
case is (expected to be) trivial, in fact, the Floer complex is null-homotopic, the only interesting
theory is the quantitative theory. Consider

Proof. Let γ′ be another loop which is homologous to γ. Choosing a concrete cobordism between

action by a constant. An AMS construction and FOP perturbation for Fγ can be transported to
Fγ′. Hence the bar-length spectrum does not depend on the representating loop γ.
□
Remark 28.2. It is standard to argue that the barcode Bγ(H) varies Lipschitz continuously with
H, where on barcodes we use the bottleneck distance. Hence the barcode Bγ(H) also extends to
degenerate Hamiltonians.
